[tool_result]
1	"""
2	Assignment 1, Part B starter -- Cascade Outdoor weekly unit forecasting.
3	
4	Everything that would otherwise differ between submissions for no good reason
5	is fixed here: the fold assignments, the polynomial bases, the penalty grids,
6	the bootstrap resamples and the query points.
7	
8	Nothing here cleans the data, estimates the floor, fits a model, chooses a
9	penalty or interprets anything.  Those are the assignment.
10	
11	Usage:
12	    from load import (load_h, load_a, load_replicates, test_extract,
13	                      dedup_h, FEATURES, TARGET, make_folds,
14	                      poly_pipeline, DEGREES, ALPHA_GRID,
15	                      bootstrap_indices, QUERY_CONFIGS, query_frame,
16	                      N_BOOTSTRAP)
17	"""
18	from pathlib import Path
19	
20	import numpy as np
21	import pandas as pd
22	from sklearn.model_selection import KFold
23	from sklearn.pipeline import Pipeline
24	from sklearn.preprocessing import PolynomialFeatures, StandardScaler
25	
26	DATA = Path(__file__).resolve().parents[1] / "data"
27	
28	TARGET = "units"
29	ID_COLS = ("store_id", "week_id")
30	
31	# The twelve recorded predictor fields, in extract order.  Both divisions
32	# record all twelve.  Whether all twelve matter is not stated anywhere.
33	FEATURES = [
34	    "promo_depth_pct", "own_price_index", "competitor_price_index",
35	    "marketing_spend_k", "temp_f", "loyalty_share", "store_sqft_k",
36	    "inventory_cover_wks", "assortment_breadth", "catalog_pages",
37	    "returns_rate", "staffing_hours",
38	]
39	
40	# ---------------------------------------------------------------- loaders
41	
42	def load_h() -> pd.DataFrame:
43	    """Hardgoods store-weeks, exactly as the pipeline delivered them.
44	
45	    Read the pipeline note in the data dictionary before you model on this.
46	    """
47	    return pd.read_csv(DATA / "division_h.csv")
48	
49	
50	def load_a() -> pd.DataFrame:
51	    """Apparel store-weeks.  Already cleaned -- see the data dictionary."""
52	    return pd.read_csv(DATA / "division_a.csv")
53	
54	
55	def load_replicates() -> pd.DataFrame:
56	    """The controlled repeat-observation extract, and the final test set.
57	
58	    Use it for two things and no others: estimating the noise floor, and
59	    scoring each tuned model exactly once at the end.
60	    """
61	    return pd.read_csv(DATA / "replicates.csv")
62	
63	
64	def test_extract(division: str) -> pd.DataFrame:
65	    """The replicate rows for one division ('H' or 'A').  Your test set.
66	
67	    Score each tuned configuration on this exactly once, at the end.
68	    """
69	    r = load_replicates()
70	    return r.loc[r["division"] == division].reset_index(drop=True)
71	
72	
73	def dedup_h(df: pd.DataFrame) -> pd.DataFrame:
74	    """The Hardgoods extract with the duplicated block removed.
75	
76	    The load behind `division_h.csv` was re-run and appended rather than
77	    replaced (ticket CO-4471), so affected store-weeks appear twice.  Use
78	    this for everything in Part B.
79	    """
80	    return df.drop_duplicates(subset=["week_id"]).reset_index(drop=True)
81	
82	
83	# ------------------------------------------------- the fixed CV protocol
84	
85	CV_SEED = 575
86	
87	
88	def make_folds(n_splits: int = 5) -> KFold:
89	    """The fold assignment every submission uses.  Do not change the seed."""
90	    return KFold(n_splits=n_splits, shuffle=True, random_state=CV_SEED)
91	
92	
93	# The flexibility dial for this assignment.  Degree 1 is the twelve recorded
94	# fields as they come; degree 2 adds every square and every pairwise product;
95	# degree 3 adds every triple.  The term counts are worth knowing before you
96	# fit anything, and `basis_size()` prints them.
97	DEGREES = (1, 2, 3)
98	
99	
100	def basis_size(degree: int, p: int = len(FEATURES)) -> int:
101	    """Number of terms in the degree-`degree` basis on `p` inputs."""
102	    from math import comb
103	    return comb(p + degree, degree) - 1
104	
105	
106	def poly_pipeline(estimator, degree: int = 2) -> Pipeline:
107	    """Degree-`degree` basis -> standardise -> estimator, as one pipeline.
108	
109	    Fitting this inside a cross-validation fold fits the expansion and the
110	    scaler on the training part of that fold only, which is the point.
111	    Passing the estimator a pre-expanded, pre-scaled matrix would leak the
112	    held-out rows into the preprocessing; see Handout 1 §7.2.
113	
114	    Standardising *before* the penalty is not optional.  Ridge penalises
115	    coefficients, so it penalises a term measured in thousands far less than
116	    the same term measured in units; on a raw polynomial basis the result is
117	    approximately OLS wearing a penalty's name.  Handout 3 §5.2.
118	    """
119	    return Pipeline([
120	        ("poly", PolynomialFeatures(degree=degree, include_bias=False)),
121	        ("scale", StandardScaler()),
122	        ("est", estimator),
123	    ])
124	
125	
126	# 25 penalties, log-spaced.  Both divisions' best value falls inside this
127	# range at every degree; if yours lands on an endpoint, something upstream
128	# is wrong.
129	ALPHA_GRID = np.logspace(-2, 4, 25)
130	
131	
132	# ------------------------------------------------------- Part B3 fixtures
133	
134	N_BOOTSTRAP = 20
135	
136	# Three configurations per division -- low, middle and high demand.  Each one
137	# also appears in replicates.csv, so the average of its ten replicate weeks is
138	# an estimate of the true mean demand there, accurate to about sigma/sqrt(10).
139	QUERY_CONFIGS = {
140	    "H": ["H43", "H16", "H01"],
141	    "A": ["A10", "A08", "A07"],
142	}
143	
144	
145	def query_frame(division: str) -> pd.DataFrame:
146	    """The three query points for a division, one row each, FEATURES columns.
147	
148	    Indexed by config_id and in the order given by QUERY_CONFIGS.
149	    """
150	    r = load_replicates()
151	    r = r.loc[r["division"] == division]
152	    first = r.groupby("config_id")[FEATURES].first()
153	    return first.loc[QUERY_CONFIGS[division]]
154	
155	
156	def bootstrap_indices(n: int, n_draws: int = N_BOOTSTRAP, seed: int = CV_SEED):
157	    """Yield `n_draws` index arrays, each a bootstrap resample of size n.
158	
159	    Fixed so that everyone's B3 resamples the same way and differences
160	    between submissions come from the modelling, not the plumbing.
161	    """
162	    rng = np.random.default_rng(seed)
163	    for _ in range(n_draws):
164	        yield rng.integers(0, n, n)
165	
166	
167	# ------------------------------------------------------------------ sanity
168	
169	if __name__ == "__main__":
170	    for name, df in (("division_h", load_h()), ("division_a", load_a())):
171	        print(f"{name:12s} rows={len(df):5,}  features={len(FEATURES)}  "
172	              f"units: mean={df[TARGET].mean():7.1f} sd={df[TARGET].std():6.1f} "
173	              f"min={df[TARGET].min():7.1f} max={df[TARGET].max():8.1f}")
174	    r = load_replicates()
175	    print(f"replicates   rows={len(r):5,}  configs={r['config_id'].nunique()}  "
176	          f"divisions={sorted(r['division'].unique())}  "
177	          f"rows per config={sorted(r.groupby('config_id').size().unique())}")
178	    print()
179	    print("terms in each basis, on 12 inputs:")
180	    for d in DEGREES:
181	        print(f"  degree {d}: {basis_size(d):5,} terms")
182	    for d in ("H", "A"):
183	        print(f"query points {d}: {QUERY_CONFIGS[d]}")

[tool_result]
1	"""
2	B3 -- the stability experiment.  The loop is written; the models are yours.
3	
4	    python3 stability.py
5	
6	What this measures.  A fitted model is a random object: it depends on which
7	training sample you happened to get (Handout 3 §2).  Refit the same procedure
8	on 20 bootstrap resamples of a division's extract, predict the same three
9	configurations each time, and you get 20 predictions per configuration.  Their
10	**spread** estimates variance.  The distance from their **average** to the
11	truth estimates bias.  The truth is available here because each query
12	configuration also appears in replicates.csv ten times, so the mean of those
13	ten estimates the true mean demand to within about sigma/sqrt(10) ~ 4 units.
14	
15	What you have to do.  Fill in `build_models()` with the four B2 models, using
16	the penalties you tuned in B2 -- do NOT re-tune inside the resamples, or you
17	will be measuring something else.  Then read the output.
18	
19	Everything else is fixed: 20 resamples from `bootstrap_indices()`, three query
20	points per division from `QUERY_CONFIGS`, the same seed for everyone.
21	"""
22	from pathlib import Path
23	
24	import numpy as np
25	import pandas as pd
26	from sklearn.linear_model import LinearRegression, Ridge  # noqa: F401
27	
28	from load import (FEATURES, N_BOOTSTRAP, QUERY_CONFIGS, TARGET,
29	                  bootstrap_indices, dedup_h, load_a, load_h, load_replicates,
30	                  poly_pipeline, query_frame)
31	
32	OUT = Path(__file__).resolve().parent / "b3_outputs"
33	
34	# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
35	# YOUR B2 PENALTIES.  Replace these with the values your own B2 tuning chose.
36	# They are per division and per degree, and they are not all the same number --
37	# at one of the two degrees the two divisions land in the same place, and at the
38	# other they do not.  Which is which is part of what B2 asked you to notice.
39	# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
40	RIDGE_ALPHA_DEG1 = {"H": None, "A": None}
41	RIDGE_ALPHA_DEG2 = {"H": None, "A": None}
42	
43	
44	def build_models(division: str) -> dict:
45	    """The four B2 models, ready to fit.  Fill this in.
46	
47	    Return a dict mapping a label to a fresh, unfitted estimator.  A fresh one
48	    every call -- if you build them once outside the loop, every resample will
49	    refit the same object and you will still get the right answer, but it is
50	    easy to get wrong in a way that silently reuses a fitted model.
51	
52	    Use `poly_pipeline(estimator, degree=d)` so that the basis and the scaler
53	    are fitted on each resample rather than once on the whole extract.
54	
55	    The four:
56	        degree-1 OLS     poly_pipeline(LinearRegression(), degree=1)
57	        degree-1 ridge   poly_pipeline(Ridge(alpha=...), degree=1)
58	        degree-2 OLS     poly_pipeline(LinearRegression(), degree=2)
59	        degree-2 ridge   poly_pipeline(Ridge(alpha=...), degree=2)
60	    """
61	    raise NotImplementedError("fill in build_models()")
62	
63	
64	# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
65	# Fixed below this line.
66	# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
67	
68	def truth_at_queries(division: str) -> np.ndarray:
69	    """Replicate-based estimate of true mean demand at the three query points."""
70	    r = load_replicates()
71	    r = r.loc[r["division"] == division]
72	    return (r.groupby("config_id")[TARGET].mean()
73	            .loc[QUERY_CONFIGS[division]].to_numpy())
74	
75	
76	def run(division: str, train: pd.DataFrame) -> pd.DataFrame:
77	    X = train[FEATURES].to_numpy()
78	    y = train[TARGET].to_numpy()
79	    Xq = query_frame(division).to_numpy()
80	    truth = truth_at_queries(division)
81	
82	    draws = {label: [] for label in build_models(division)}
83	    for idx in bootstrap_indices(len(X), N_BOOTSTRAP):
84	        for label, model in build_models(division).items():
85	            model.fit(X[idx], y[idx])
86	            draws[label].append(model.predict(Xq))
87	
88	    rows = []
89	    for label, preds in draws.items():
90	        P = np.asarray(preds)                       # (N_BOOTSTRAP, 3)
91	        sd = P.std(axis=0, ddof=1)                  # variance, per query point
92	        bias = P.mean(axis=0) - truth               # bias, per query point
93	        rows.append({
94	            "division": division,
95	            "model": label,
96	            **{f"sd_q{i + 1}": v for i, v in enumerate(sd)},
97	            **{f"bias_q{i + 1}": v for i, v in enumerate(bias)},
98	            "mean_sd": sd.mean(),
99	            "mean_abs_bias": np.abs(bias).mean(),
100	        })
101	    return pd.DataFrame(rows)
102	
103	
104	def figure(tables: pd.DataFrame) -> None:
105	    """One compact figure: mean spread against mean |bias|, by division."""
106	    import matplotlib
107	    matplotlib.use("Agg")
108	    import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
109	
110	    fig, axes = plt.subplots(1, 2, figsize=(11, 4.2), sharey=False)
111	    for ax, d in zip(axes, ("H", "A")):
112	        t = tables.loc[tables.division == d]
113	        ax.scatter(t.mean_abs_bias, t.mean_sd)
114	        for _, r in t.iterrows():
115	            ax.annotate(r.model, (r.mean_abs_bias, r.mean_sd),
116	                        textcoords="offset points", xytext=(6, 4), fontsize=8)
117	        ax.set_xlabel("mean |bias| across the three query points")
118	        ax.set_ylabel("mean sd of the 20 predictions")
119	        ax.set_title({"H": "Hardgoods", "A": "Apparel"}[d])
120	    fig.suptitle("B3: what flexibility and what a penalty do to bias and variance")
121	    fig.tight_layout()
122	    OUT.mkdir(exist_ok=True)
123	    fig.savefig(OUT / "b3_stability.png", dpi=150)
124	    print(f"wrote {OUT / 'b3_stability.png'}")
125	
126	
127	def main() -> None:
128	    tables = pd.concat([run("H", dedup_h(load_h())), run("A", load_a())],
129	                       ignore_index=True)
130	    cols = ["division", "model", "mean_sd", "mean_abs_bias"]
131	    print(tables[cols].to_string(index=False, float_format=lambda v: f"{v:8.2f}"))
132	    OUT.mkdir(exist_ok=True)
133	    tables.to_csv(OUT / "b3_stability.csv", index=False)
134	    print(f"\nwrote {OUT / 'b3_stability.csv'}")
135	    figure(tables)
136	    print()
137	    print("Three questions, each answerable from a number in that table:")
138	    print("  1. What did raising the degree do to bias, and to variance,")
139	    print("     in each division?")
140	    print("  2. What did the penalty do to each, at each degree?")
141	    print("  3. The same four procedures, the same twelve fields, two")
142	    print("     divisions. Why is the answer not the same in both?")
143	
144	
145	if __name__ == "__main__":
146	    main()
147

1	"""
2	Optional, no marks, about two minutes.  Run it and look at the two figures.
3	
4	    python3 sweep.py
5	
6	Panel 1 turns the flexibility dial by hand: degree 1, 2 and 3 on the same
7	twelve fields, fitted by unpenalised least squares, with training error and
8	5-fold cross-validated error plotted against the number of terms in the basis.
9	Handout 3 §1.4 describes the shape you are about to see; the point of running it
10	is that the two divisions do not produce the same shape, and the reason is
11	sample size.
12	
13	Panel 2 turns the same dial the other way, continuously: degree-2 ridge across
14	the whole penalty grid.  Large alpha is a rigid model, small alpha a flexible
15	one, and the minimum of the cross-validated curve is what B2 tunes to.
16	
17	Midterm 1 will ask you to read a plot like these.  Worth doing before the exam
18	even if you have not started Part B.
19	"""
20	from pathlib import Path
21	
22	import matplotlib
23	matplotlib.use("Agg")
24	import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
25	import numpy as np
26	from sklearn.linear_model import LinearRegression, Ridge
27	from sklearn.metrics import mean_squared_error
28	from sklearn.model_selection import cross_val_score
29	
30	from load import (ALPHA_GRID, DEGREES, FEATURES, TARGET, basis_size, dedup_h,
31	                  load_a, load_h, make_folds, poly_pipeline)
32	
33	OUT = Path(__file__).resolve().parent / "sweep_outputs"
34	CV = make_folds()
35	
36	
37	def degree_sweep(X, y):
38	    train, cv = [], []
39	    for d in DEGREES:
40	        pipe = poly_pipeline(LinearRegression(), degree=d)
41	        s = cross_val_score(pipe, X, y, scoring="neg_mean_squared_error",
42	                            cv=CV, n_jobs=-1)
43	        cv.append(-s.mean())
44	        pipe.fit(X, y)
45	        train.append(mean_squared_error(y, pipe.predict(X)))
46	    return np.array(train), np.array(cv)
47	
48	
49	def alpha_sweep(X, y):
50	    cv = []
51	    for a in ALPHA_GRID:
52	        s = cross_val_score(poly_pipeline(Ridge(alpha=a), degree=2), X, y,
53	                            scoring="neg_mean_squared_error", cv=CV, n_jobs=-1)
54	        cv.append(-s.mean())
55	    return np.array(cv)
56	
57	
58	def main():
59	    OUT.mkdir(exist_ok=True)
60	    divisions = (("Hardgoods", dedup_h(load_h())), ("Apparel", load_a()))
61	    terms = [basis_size(d) for d in DEGREES]
62	
63	    fig, axes = plt.subplots(1, 2, figsize=(11, 4.2))
64	    for ax, (name, df) in zip(axes, divisions):
65	        X, y = df[FEATURES].to_numpy(), df[TARGET].to_numpy()
66	        train, cv = degree_sweep(X, y)
67	        ax.plot(terms, train, "o-", label="training MSE")
68	        ax.plot(terms, cv, "s-", label="5-fold CV MSE")
69	        ax.set_xscale("log")
70	        ax.set_xticks(terms)
71	        ax.set_xticklabels([f"deg {d}\n{t} terms" for d, t in zip(DEGREES, terms)])
72	        ax.set_title(f"{name}  (n = {len(df):,})")
73	        ax.set_ylabel("MSE")
74	        ax.legend()
75	        for d, t, c in zip(DEGREES, terms, cv):
76	            ax.annotate(f"{c:,.0f}", (t, c), textcoords="offset points",
77	                        xytext=(0, 8), ha="center", fontsize=8)
78	    fig.suptitle("Turning the flexibility dial by hand: unpenalised least squares")
79	    fig.tight_layout()
80	    fig.savefig(OUT / "degree_sweep.png", dpi=150)
81	
82	    fig, axes = plt.subplots(1, 2, figsize=(11, 4.2))
83	    for ax, (name, df) in zip(axes, divisions):
84	        X, y = df[FEATURES].to_numpy(), df[TARGET].to_numpy()
85	        cv = alpha_sweep(X, y)
86	        ax.plot(ALPHA_GRID, cv, "-")
87	        best = int(np.argmin(cv))
88	        ax.plot(ALPHA_GRID[best], cv[best], "o")
89	        ax.annotate(f"alpha = {ALPHA_GRID[best]:.3g}\nCV = {cv[best]:,.0f}",
90	                    (ALPHA_GRID[best], cv[best]), textcoords="offset points",
91	                    xytext=(10, 10), fontsize=8)
92	        ax.set_xscale("log")
93	        ax.set_xlabel("ridge penalty alpha   (rigid <-- ... --> flexible)")
94	        ax.set_ylabel("5-fold CV MSE")
95	        ax.set_title(f"{name}  (n = {len(df):,})")
96	    fig.suptitle("The same dial, continuously: degree-2 ridge across the penalty grid")
97	    fig.tight_layout()
98	    fig.savefig(OUT / "alpha_sweep.png", dpi=150)
99	
100	    print(f"wrote {OUT / 'degree_sweep.png'}")
101	    print(f"wrote {OUT / 'alpha_sweep.png'}")
102	    print()
103	    print("Two things to notice, and one question to take to Part B:")
104	    print("  * where the cross-validated curve turns up, and whether it turns")
105	    print("    up at all inside the range plotted, in each division;")
106	    print("  * how far the best penalty is from zero in each division.")
107	    print("  * the two divisions record the same twelve fields, by the same")
108	    print("    job, for the same company. So what is actually different?")
109	
110	
111	if __name__ == "__main__":
112	    main()
113

521	# Assignment 1 — Evaluation and flexibility
522	
523	**IDS 575: Machine Learning and Statistical Methods for Business Analytics · Fall 2026**
524	Released Thursday, September 3 · **Due Thursday, October 1, 6:00 PM Central** · 10% of course grade
525	
526	Covers Handouts 1–3: supervised learning and loss, training versus test error, cross-validation, choosing a split that matches deployment, logistic regression, model flexibility, bias and variance, and regularization.
527	
528	**Expected effort: 6–8 focused hours.** Part A is the larger of the two parts.
529	
530	Two independent cases, one memo.
531	
532	- **Part A — Northline Fitness.** Build an evaluation for a churn model that reflects how the model would actually be used, and turn its predictions into a targeting decision priced in dollars. Uses Handouts 1–2.
533	- **Part B — Cascade Outdoor.** Measure how much of the forecast error in two retail divisions is unavoidable, compare four models against that floor, and say what a company-wide modelling standard should specify. Uses Handout 3.
534	
535	Part A can be started the day the assignment is released. Part B needs Handout 3, covered in Lecture 4; you will have at least two weeks after that. Midterm 1 on September 24 covers Lectures 1–4, so this assignment is direct preparation for it, and the deadline falls a week after the exam.
536	
537	---
538	
539	# Part A — Northline Fitness
540	
541	## The situation
542	
543	Northline Fitness sells a subscription fitness app. Members pay monthly on one of three plans. About 9% of active members cancel in any 60-day window.
544	
545	The retention team wants to run a monthly program: score every active member for the probability they cancel within 60 days, and send the highest-scoring members a **retention offer** — one month at 50% off plus a personal check-in.
546	
547	Finance has priced it:
548	
549	| Quantity | Value |
550	|---|---|
551	| Cost of extending one offer | **$18** |
552	| Lifetime value of a member who stays | **$240** |
553	| Fraction of would-be churners retained by the offer | **0.35** |
554	| Effect on a member who was not going to churn | none; the $18 is still spent |
555	
556	So an offer to a member who would have churned is worth `240 × 0.35 - 18 = $66`. An offer to a member who would not have churned costs `$18`. No offer is worth `$0`.
557	
558	A junior analyst has already pulled 24 months of history and fitted a first model: logistic regression on all available columns, 5-fold cross-validation, encoders and scalers fitted inside the folds. It reports **0.967 AUC**. The code is in `part1_northline/starter/analyst_model.py` and runs as-is.
559	
560	The VP of Membership wants a second opinion before this goes to the operating committee. **Your job is to produce the estimate she can plan against, and a recommendation.**
561	
562	## How the model would be deployed
563	
564	If approved, the program runs monthly from **April 2026**, scoring the active book at the start of each month. Two things about that book differ from the history you have:
565	
566	1. **Corporate members.** Northline signed three employer wellness contracts starting February 2025. The `corporate` channel is 0% of the book in 2024, about 20% by December 2025, and Membership Analytics forecasts **roughly 35% of the April–June 2026 book**.
567	2. **A competitor** launched a rival app in July 2025 and is still spending heavily.
568	
569	## What you need to know about the data
570	
571	All of this is also in `part1_northline/data/DATA_DICTIONARY.md`. None of it is hidden; the work is in handling it correctly, not in finding it.
572	
573	- **Grain.** One row per member per calendar month. A member appears in many rows. The 4,200 members produce 33,340 rows.
574	- **Timing.** The dictionary states, for every column, the window over which it is measured. Some columns are recorded **after** the month being scored, and so would not exist at the moment the April 2026 model has to produce a score. Use the timing notes to decide which columns are admissible; keeping a column is a decision you should be able to justify just as much as dropping one.
575	- **Duplicated rows (ticket NLF-2231).** The March 2025 load was re-run after a partition failure and appended instead of replacing. Every affected row appears twice, identical on every column. Remove the duplicates before modelling.
576	- **Date formats (ticket NLF-2198).** `snapshot_month` appears in three formats. `load.py` provides `month_key()` to normalise them.
577	
578	## A1 — Baselines (before any modelling)
579	
580	Report, in one small table:
581

700	## Ground rules
701	
702	- **Score each test set once.** Northline's test period and Cascade's replicate extract are used at the end and select nothing.
703	- **State every Part B error against the floor.** A raw MSE has no interpretation on its own.
704	- **Every number in the memo must be reproducible** from your submitted code, with one command.
705	
706	## Two practical habits
707	
708	**Do not let your agent read a CSV into the conversation.** `member_months.csv` is 33,000 rows; pasting it can cost more in one message than a whole working session should. Have the agent write code that reads the file and reports numbers back. The data dictionaries are short and are meant to be read.
709	
710	**Start a fresh session for each work block** — the audit table, then the threshold, then Part B — rather than one session running all month. It keeps costs down and makes your transcripts easier to export and read. `../00_start_here/05_setting_up_your_agent.md` has the rest.

1023	Two nits, low stakes:
1024	
1025	- [.gitignore](.gitignore) is the stock Python template, which ignores any directory named `lib/`, `var/`, `build/`, or `target/` at any depth. If you ever name an `analysis/lib/` helper folder, it would silently not be committed, and the submission would not run on a clean copy.
1026	- [part2_cascade/starter/sweep.py](part2_cascade/starter/sweep.py) and [stability.py](part2_cascade/starter/stability.py) write to `sweep_outputs/` and `b3_outputs/` next to themselves, not to `analysis/outputs/`. Your own rule sends figures and saved numbers to `analysis/outputs/`, so your Part B code will want to override those paths.
1027	
1028	## On the VS Code workspace question
1029	
1030	**No, this is not a workspace, and you do not need one.** You have a single folder open. The [.vscode/settings.json](.vscode/settings.json) file here is folder-level configuration (it just sets the Python environment manager), which is a different thing from a workspace.
1031	
1032	A VS Code *workspace* proper means a `.code-workspace` file: a small JSON file listing one or more root folders plus settings that apply across them. Its purpose is multi-root, letting you open, say, a frontend repo and a backend repo in one window, sharing search, settings, and a single terminal. You have no `.code-workspace` file anywhere on your Desktop or home directory, so every window you open is a plain single-folder window.
1033	
1034	For this assignment, single-folder is the better setup. Policy §3 restricts the work to this folder, and a multi-root workspace would put other course folders in the same window where search, the terminal, and I could all reach them. The one case where a workspace would help is if you wanted the course repo's handouts open beside this folder for reference. Since the handouts are not in this folder and the README points to them in the course repo, you may want them nearby, but I would open them in a separate window rather than joining the roots.
1035	
1036	What is the topic for this work block?

1400	
1401	## 1. Python environment, both machines
1402	
1403	Created `.venv` in the project root and installed into it. Pinned versions are now in [requirements.txt](requirements.txt).
1404	
1405	```
1406	numpy 2.4.6 · scipy 1.17.1 · pandas 3.0.5 · scikit-learn 1.9.1 · matplotlib 3.11.2
1407	```
1408	
1409	On the Mac, install Python 3.11 first (`brew install python@3.11`, or the installer from python.org), then:
1410	
1411	```
1412	python3.11 -m venv .venv
1413	source .venv/bin/activate
1414	pip install -r requirements.txt
1415	```
1416	
1417	Three things that matter for the two-machine setup:
1418	
1419	- **`.venv` itself never travels.** It contains Windows `.exe` files and absolute paths. It is git-ignored, and it must stay that way. `requirements.txt` is the thing that travels, and it is tracked.
1420	- **Versions are pinned on purpose.** scikit-learn and numpy can shift numbers slightly between releases. Pinning means an AUC computed on the Windows machine reproduces on the Mac. If you ever change a version, change it in `requirements.txt` and re-run everything.
1421	- **Match the Python minor version too.** 3.11 on both. The brief only requires 3.10+, but mixing 3.11 and 3.13 is a needless source of drift.
1422	
1423	I smoke-tested the starters under the new environment rather than assuming they work, since pandas 3.0 is a major release. All clean: `load_raw()` returns 33,340 rows and 18 columns, `month_key()` normalises, `load_h()` returns 3,350 rows which `dedup_h()` reduces to 3,000, `load_a()` 197, `replicates.csv` 900, `make_folds()` gives 5 folds, `ALPHA_GRID` has 25 values, `QUERY_CONFIGS` has H and A, `poly_pipeline()` builds `poly → scale → est`, and `bootstrap_indices()` yields 20 draws. No data contents were read into this conversation, only shapes.
1424

1426	
1427	Edited. The stock Python template ignored `lib/`, `var/`, `build/`, `dist/`, `target/`, `instance/`, `cover/` and similar at **any** depth, so an `analysis/part2/lib/` would have vanished silently from the submission. Those patterns are now anchored to the repo root (`/lib/`, `/var/`, and so on), which is what the template intends anyway. I also added an exception so run logs saved under `analysis/outputs/` are committable, since `*.log` was being ignored.

1820	[tool_result]
1821	# Exporting your AI session history
1822	
1823	**IDS 575: Machine Learning and Statistical Methods for Business Analytics · Fall 2026**
1824	
1825	Every assignment requires the complete exported history of every AI session you used. This sheet
1826	explains how, for both approved agents.
1827	
1828	> **Export at the end of each working session, not at the end of the assignment.** Both tools have
1829	> some way of losing history — a retention window, a clipboard that chokes on a long conversation.
1830	> If you wait until the night before the deadline, some of what you need may no longer exist.
1831	
1832	> **Before you submit, open your export and read it.** Agent transcripts embed the full contents of
1833	> files the agent read and the full output of commands it ran. Work in a clean folder that contains
1834	> only course material.
1835	
1836	---
1837	
1838	## What goes in `sessions/`
1839	
1840	```
1841	sessions/
1842	  README.md              which agent, how many sessions, anything the export omits
1843	  codex/                 one file per session
1844	  claude-code/           one file per session
1845	```
1846	
1847	You will normally have only one of those two subfolders. `sessions/README.md` is required either way
1848	and can be three lines.
1849	
1850	---
1851	
1852	## 1. Codex
1853	
1854	### The app
1855	
1856	Open the session, use **Copy as Markdown** from the conversation menu, and paste into a file:
1857	
1858	```
1859	sessions/codex/a1-session-1.md
1860	```
1861	
1862	That is the whole procedure. Name the files in the order you worked; the names do not matter beyond
1863	being distinguishable.
1864	
1865	Two cautions:
1866	
1867	- **It goes to the clipboard, not to a file.** Paste it somewhere immediately. A plain text editor is
1868	  safer than Word, which has been reported to struggle with very long conversations.
1869	- **Do it per session, while the session is open.** There is no bulk export.
1870	
1871	### The CLI
1872	
1873	Inside a session, `/copy` puts the conversation on the clipboard; paste it into a `.md` file the same
1874	way.
1875	
1876	### The transcript on disk — your backup
1877	
1878	Codex also writes a full rollout file per session:
1879	
1880	```
1881	~/.codex/sessions/YYYY/MM/DD/rollout-<timestamp>-<session-uuid>.jsonl
1882	```
1883	
1884	Copy them if you want a belt-and-braces record:
1885	
1886	```bash
1887	mkdir -p sessions/codex
1888	cp ~/.codex/sessions/2026/09/11/rollout-*.jsonl sessions/codex/
1889	```
1890	
1891	This is the complete log — more complete than the Markdown copy, which renders the conversation
1892	rather than recording every tool call. **The Markdown is what I ask for. The JSONL is what saves you
1893	if the Markdown copy failed and you did not notice.** Submitting both is fine and never penalised.
1894	
1895	Two settings to check before you start:
1896	
1897	- If `~/.codex/config.toml` contains `[history] persistence = "none"`, your history is being
1898	  suppressed. Set it back to `"save-all"` (the default).
1899	- Do not run assignment work with `--ephemeral`, which skips session persistence.
1900	
1901	**Check the size before you attach.** Rollout files can get large, because they store repeated
1902	context snapshots and raw tool output:
1903	
1904	```bash
1905	du -h sessions/codex/*
1906	```
1907	
1908	If the folder is over about 50 MB, zip it and say so in `sessions/README.md`.
1909	
1910	---
1911	
1912	## 2. Claude Code
1913	
1914	### Option A — the raw transcript
1915	
1916	Transcripts are stored as JSONL at:
1917	
1918	```
1919	~/.claude/projects/<encoded-project-path>/<session-id>.jsonl
1920	```
1921	
1922	`<encoded-project-path>` is your working directory with every non-alphanumeric character replaced by
1923	`-`. Run `/status` inside a session to get the session ID; that ID is the filename. To see every
1924	session you have, `claude --resume` opens an interactive picker.
1925	
1926	### Option B — the readable export
1927	
1928	From inside a session: `/export sessions/claude-code/a1-session-1.txt`
1929	
1930	### Retention
1931	
1932	Claude Code deletes transcripts after **30 days** by default. Copy the file into your assignment
1933	folder at the end of each session rather than relying on the retention setting.
1934	
1935	---
1936	
1937	## `sessions/README.md` — templates
1938	
1939	Ordinary case:
1940	
1941	```markdown
1942	# AI sessions for Assignment 1
1943	
1944	Agent used: Codex app (4 sessions)
1945	
1946	- codex/ : 4 Markdown exports, complete. Rollout JSONL for the same four sessions
1947	           also included.
1948	```
1949	
1950	With a gap:
1951	
1952	```markdown
1953	# AI sessions for Assignment 1
1954	
1955	Agent used: Claude Code (5 sessions)
1956	
1957	- claude-code/ : 4 JSONL transcripts, complete. A fifth session on 18 September
1958	                 was lost before I exported it; it was about half an hour, spent
1959	                 debugging the grouped-fold split, and its result is in run_a2.py.
1960	```
1961

1967	**do not skip the export** — find the equivalent, do it, and say in `sessions/README.md` what you did
1968	instead. Reporting that a documented step does not work is helpful and will not cost you marks.

`dbm.ndbm` changed database backends in Homebrew Python 3.11.
If you need to read a database from a previous Homebrew Python created via `dbm.ndbm`,

Good questions, and the last point is noted: no scripts until the concepts are clear. Let me take them in order. I'll fix the VS Code setting as part of answering the second one.

## 1. `__pycache__` — harmless, and why it got hunted anyway

When Python imports a module (`from load import ...`), it compiles the `.py` source into *bytecode* — a lower-level instruction format the interpreter executes directly — and caches that in `__pycache__/load.cpython-311.pyc` so the next import skips recompilation. It is purely a cache: deleting it costs nothing, it regenerates automatically, and it never changes results.

So it is harmless to the analysis. The Windows session removed it for a narrower reason: you asked whether the supplied folders were untouched, and `__pycache__/` was a physical addition inside `part1_northline/starter/`. It was git-ignored, so it would never have shipped in the submission, but "the supplied folders contain exactly what was issued" is a cleaner statement than "plus some ignored cache files". That is a tidiness standard, not a correctness one. Going forward I will not treat it as a problem — if the folders pick up `__pycache__` from our own imports, that is fine and git will ignore it.

## 5. `-B` and bytecode (same topic)

`python -B script.py` tells Python not to write those `.pyc` files, and `PYTHONDONTWRITEBYTECODE=1` does the same via an environment variable. I used it in the smoke test only for the tidiness reason above. Given your answer to question 1, I'd rather drop `-B` entirely: the one-command `run.sh` should be plain `python`, because the grader will run it that way and there is no reason to make our command unusual.

## 2. VS Code: activate `.venv` automatically

The current [.vscode/settings.json](.vscode/settings.json) actually works *against* you: `"python-envs.defaultEnvManager": "ms-python.python:system"` tells the Python extension to prefer the system interpreter (your 3.14), not `.venv`. Changing it to the `venv` manager makes the extension look for `.venv` in the workspace root, which exists on both machines. I'm not hardcoding an interpreter path because it differs by OS (`.venv/bin/python` here, `.venv\Scripts\python.exe` on Windows); auto-detection handles both.

## 3. "Shuffles time instead of fitting past → future"

Look at [analyst_model.py:77](part1_northline/starter/analyst_model.py#L77): `StratifiedKFold(n_splits=5, shuffle=True, random_state=0)`. It takes all 33,340 member-months from Jan 2024 to Dec 2025, shuffles them randomly, and deals them into five folds. Each fold is held out in turn while the model is fitted on the other four.

That means when the model is being *tested* on a row from, say, March 2025, its *training* data contained rows from June, September, December 2025 — the future relative to the row being scored. Two reasons that inflates the number:

- **The deployment question is different.** In April 2026 the model will be fitted on history and asked about members whose future is unknown. The honest estimate of that is: fit on months up to some cutoff, evaluate on months after it. A random shuffle instead estimates "how well can I interpolate among months I've already seen" — an easier question the business never asks.
- **The world drifts.** The competitor launched July 2025, corporate members ramp from 0% to 20% over 2025. A shuffle lets the model learn those late-2025 patterns and then get credit for "predicting" them. A past→future split forces it to face them the way the deployed model will: cold.

This is Handout 1 §7.3 ("splits that match deployment"). The brief's fixed windows in [load.py:36-38](part1_northline/starter/load.py#L36-L38) encode exactly this: train ≤ 2025-06, validation 2025-09, test 2025-10 → 12.

A closely related but separate problem is A2's change 2: with a shuffle, the same member's January and February rows land in different folds, so the model sees member 1234's history in training and is then "tested" on member 1234's next month. Members are sticky; that's near-memorisation. Grouping by member fixes that; splitting by time fixes the other. The brief has you do them one at a time so you can see how much each moves the number.

## 4. Base rate

Simpler than you're describing. The base rate is just **the fraction of rows where `churn_next_60d = 1`** — the share of member-months that end in a cancellation within 60 days. The brief tells you to expect about 9%.

Your phrasing ("if we randomly reached out to x% of customers, how many would still churn") is actually a *use* of the base rate rather than its definition, and it's a good one to hold onto for A3: if you send offers to a random 10% of the book, about 9% of those recipients are churners. That is the number a model has to beat — the precision you get with zero information. It also explains why "predict no churn for everyone" scores ~91% accuracy while being useless: accuracy just reports `1 − base rate` when the positives are rare (Handout R1 §4.2). That's what A1 item 2 wants you to say in one sentence.

One nuance for later: it's a row-level rate. A member who stays two years contributes 24 rows of `0`; a member who churns after three months contributes 3 rows, the last one or two being `1`. So the member-level rate ("what fraction of members ever churn") is higher. The analyst's model works on rows, so rows is the right unit for the comparison — but you'll want to be able to say that out loud if asked.

## 6. Concepts before code

Understood. For the rest of the session, before any script, I'll state: what the number is, what it estimates, which handout section it comes from, what we expect it to be, and what would make it suspicious. Then you decide, then I write. Nothing about the design gets chosen by default.

Want me to do that walk-through now?

## Member-level vs row-level rate

Take a toy book of two members observed over a year:

| Member | Months on book | Rows | Rows with `churn_next_60d = 1` |
|---|---|---|---|
| A | stays all 12 months | 12 | 0 |
| B | cancels in April | 4 | 2 (Feb and Mar rows, since April is within 60 days of their end) |

- **Row-level rate:** 2 churn-flagged rows out of 16 = **12.5%**. This is what "base rate of `churn_next_60d`" means and what the analyst's model sees.
- **Member-level rate:** 1 of 2 members ever churned = **50%**.

The row-level rate is lower because loyal members keep generating `0` rows month after month, while a churner generates only a handful of rows and then disappears from the file (the dictionary says members "stop appearing after the month in which they cancelled"). Each `1` is diluted by all the `0`s from long-tenured members. So the same dataset gives ~9% per row and something considerably higher per member. Neither is wrong; they answer different questions. "What fraction of the *book this month* will cancel within 60 days" is the row-level question, and that is the one the retention program cares about because it scores the book monthly.

## K-fold cross-validation, briefly

The problem it solves: you want to know how well a model predicts data it was *not* fitted on, but if you hold out a big test set you've thrown away training data, and if you hold out a small one the estimate is noisy.

The procedure, with k = 5:

1. Split the rows into 5 roughly equal folds.
2. Round 1: fit the model on folds 2–5, predict fold 1, record the score.
3. Round 2: fit on folds 1, 3–5, predict fold 2. And so on through round 5.
4. Every row has now been predicted exactly once by a model that never saw it. Pool those predictions (the "out-of-fold" predictions — `oof` in [analyst_model.py:78](part1_northline/starter/analyst_model.py#L78)) and compute one AUC on them, or average the five per-fold scores.

What it estimates (Handout 1 §6): the expected error of *this fitting procedure* on new data drawn the same way the folds were drawn. That last clause is the whole assignment. Three knobs in "the same way":

- **Stratified** (what the analyst used): each fold has the same ~9% churn share. Sensible for rare outcomes; not the problem.
- **Shuffle = True:** rows are dealt to folds at random. So "new data" means "a random row from 2024–2025" — including rows from the future and rows from a member already in training. That is the mismatch with deployment.
- **Grouped / temporal folds** (A2 changes 2 and 3): deal to folds by member, or cut by month, so "new data" means "a member never seen" or "a month not yet happened".

And the standard error (Handout 1 §6, used in B2): the five per-fold scores vary; their spread divided by √5 is roughly how much you'd trust the average. Two models whose CV scores differ by less than that are not distinguishable — which is what the one-standard-error rule in B2 uses.

One more thing to hold onto: CV is for *choosing* things (features, penalties, models). Once you've chosen by looking at CV scores, the CV score is optimistic about that choice, which is why the brief also reserves a test window that gets scored once and selects nothing.

ASSISTANT  [2026-09-17T20:48:32.632Z]

Yes, five separate fits — and this is the point people trip on, so it's worth being precise.

Each round creates a fresh pipeline (`model = analyst_pipeline(...)` inside the loop at [analyst_model.py:80](part1_northline/starter/analyst_model.py#L80)), fits it on 4 folds, predicts the held-out 1, and then discards it. Five fits, five sets of coefficients, none of them kept.

What you keep is the **score**, and it belongs to the *procedure*, not to any one of those five models. "0.967 cross-validated AUC" means: "if I follow this recipe — these columns, this preprocessing, logistic regression — on data like this, I can expect roughly this AUC on rows I haven't seen." It is an estimate of how good the recipe is.

Then, if you decide to deploy, you fit **one more time on all the data** and ship that sixth model. You never measured that specific model — you can't, there's no held-out data left — but CV told you what to expect from the recipe that produced it, and a model fitted on more data is expected to do at least as well.

That is exactly the Part B protocol in the brief: **Tune** (5-fold CV to pick the penalty) → **Refit** on all training rows → **Test** once on the held-out extract. The CV fits are throwaway; the refit is the model; the test is the one honest measurement of it.

One consequence for A2: when the brief says "from change 3 onward it is measured on the test window", the fold structure goes away — you fit once on the training months and score once on the test months. Before that, the numbers are cross-validated on the building data. That's why the table has to label each row as one or the other.

The key distinction is between two kinds of things a model has:

- **Parameters** — the coefficients. The fitting algorithm chooses these by minimizing loss on the training data. Logistic regression and OLS do this for you.
- **Choices the fitting algorithm can't make for itself** — which columns go in, what polynomial degree, how big the ridge penalty is. These are decided *before* fitting. Handout 3 calls this the flexibility dial.

Why can't training error decide these? Because more flexibility always fits the training data at least as well. Degree 2 (90 terms) will always beat degree 1 (12 terms) on training MSE; a penalty of zero will always beat any positive penalty on training MSE. If you let training error choose, it chooses maximum flexibility every time, and that's the overfit model. You need a score on data the fit didn't see — and that is what CV produces.

So the mechanism is: **every candidate choice is a different recipe; CV scores each recipe; you pick the recipe with the best score.**

Concretely, for the ridge penalty in Part B. `ALPHA_GRID` has 25 values. For each one:

```
for alpha in ALPHA_GRID:                       # 25 candidates
    recipe = poly_pipeline(Ridge(alpha=alpha), degree=2)
    cv_mse[alpha] = mean of 5 held-out fold MSEs for this recipe
best_alpha = the alpha with the lowest cv_mse
```

That's 25 × 5 = 125 throwaway fits, producing a curve of CV MSE against alpha — the second panel `sweep.py` draws. The curve is U-shaped: tiny alpha ≈ unpenalised OLS (overfits, high variance), huge alpha ≈ predict the mean (underfits, high bias), and the minimum is somewhere inside. That minimum is the tuned penalty. Then you refit once on all rows with that alpha.

Same idea for features, which is what A2 is really about in spirit: "all 14 columns" and "admissible columns only" are two recipes, and you compare their held-out scores. (In A2 the comparison is driven by the timing argument rather than by which score is higher — you would drop `winback_offer_sent` even if it *helped* CV, because it won't exist at scoring time. But the scoring machinery is the same.)

Two things that follow, both of which the brief leans on:

**The winner's CV score is optimistic.** You looked at 25 scores and took the smallest. Some of that smallness is real, some is luck — the alpha that happened to suit these particular folds. So the CV score of the chosen recipe overstates how it will do on new data. That's why the protocol keeps a test set that plays no part in choosing: it gives one number that wasn't cherry-picked.

**The one-standard-error rule** (Handout 1 §6.5). Because each CV score has noise (the spread across the 5 folds ÷ √5), the minimum of the curve may not be meaningfully lower than its neighbours. The rule says: take the *simplest* recipe whose CV score is within one standard error of the best. In Part B that matters because Apparel's standard errors are large (197 rows, ~40 per fold), so several alphas — and possibly both degrees — may be statistically tied, which is the whole argument for a company-wide standard.

Nearly. Two corrections, one of order and one of vocabulary, then you have it.

**Order: refit first, then test.** The sequence is

1. **Tune.** For each candidate recipe (model type × settings × columns), run 5-fold CV on the *training* data, average the 5 held-out scores. Compare the averages, pick the winner. All those fits are discarded.
2. **Refit.** Fit the winning recipe once on *all* the training data. This is the model.
3. **Test.** Score that one model once on the test set. Report it. Don't go back.

You had test before refit; it's the other way. The test score has to be a measurement of the model you'd actually ship, so the ship-candidate has to exist first. Also note "all data" in the refit means all *training* data — the test rows never enter any fit. (In production you might later refit including the test rows to squeeze out a bit more, but then you no longer have a measurement of that model, so for this assignment you don't.)

**Vocabulary.** What CV chooses among is usually called **hyperparameters** (the ridge alpha, the degree, the column set) — the settings you fix before fitting. "Parameters" is reserved for the coefficients the fit produces. Worth keeping the two words separate because the midterm will.

And the name for the number from step 3: the **test error** (or test AUC, test MSE), also called the **held-out estimate** or the **honest estimate of generalisation error**. The brief calls it "the final estimate". "Unbiased" is roughly right and here's the precise version: the CV score of the *winner* is biased upward because you selected the best of 25 noisy numbers; the test score is not, because nothing was chosen by looking at it. That is the entire reason the test set exists and why "score once, select nothing" is a hard rule in your CLAUDE.md.

So your sentence, corrected:

> I choose the recipe — model type, hyperparameters, columns — by comparing the CV averages, knowing the winner's average is optimistic. I refit the chosen recipe on all training data. I score that one model once on the test set, and that test score is the honest estimate I report.

One last piece that ties it to Part A: for Northline, the "test set" is the 2025-10 → 12 window, the "training data" is ≤ 2025-06, and the brief adds a **validation month** (2025-09) as the place where "any choice made by looking at data" happens. That's just a one-fold version of step 1 — same job as CV, done with a single time-ordered holdout because a random 5-fold split would put the future in training.

Honest answer first: **I can't verify what the professor said, because the handouts aren't in this folder** — that's open item 3 in `NOTES.md` (pull Handouts 1–3 and R1 from Canvas). Handout 1 §6 is where this would be, and you should check it rather than take my word. What I can do is tell you the standard position, show you what the brief itself does, and give you the reading of the professor's warning that is consistent with both.

**The standard position.** Choosing the model family by CV is not *wrong*, but it is not free. Every choice you make by looking at CV scores — 25 alphas, 3 degrees, 3 model families — adds a candidate to the pool, and the winner's CV score is optimistic in proportion to how many candidates you compared and how noisy the scores are. Pick the best of 75 recipes and you've almost certainly picked one that got lucky on these folds. Two consequences:

1. **The CV score of the winner is not your estimate.** The test score is. That holds whether you selected an alpha or a family; the family choice just makes the optimism larger.
2. **The more you decide in advance, the less you need to select.** If the problem tells you it's a binary outcome with a dollar-valued decision, you don't need CV to tell you logistic regression is the model. CV is then only doing the small job it does well: tuning within a family.

The most likely form of the professor's point, and the one I'd write on the midterm: *decide the model class from the problem, not from the data; use CV to tune what you can't decide in advance; and never let the test set touch any of it.* The purest version — "decide the model before seeing any data" — is the safe extreme of the same principle. The only way to select a family by CV *and* still get an honest score is nested CV (an outer loop that holds out data the whole selection process never saw), which is exactly the tune/refit/test protocol with the test set playing the outer role.

**Check it against the brief, which is the professor's own design.** Both parts fix the model class up front:

- Part A: logistic regression, full stop. Every A2 change is about the *evaluation*, never about the model. The brief doesn't ask you to try a random forest and never rewards it.
- Part B: "There are no marks for a better model than the four specified below." The four cells are fixed in advance; CV picks only alpha within each. B2 question 2 does compare the four specifications by CV score — but note *how*: through the one-standard-error rule, i.e. the question is "are these distinguishable at all?", not "which won?". And the final numbers are the test MSEs, scored once.

So the brief is already doing what the warning says: class chosen before data, CV tunes the penalty, comparisons across specifications are made with their uncertainty attached, and the test set settles it. Whatever I write for you will follow that structure and nothing looser.

When you pull the handouts, look at Handout 1 §6 (CV, what it estimates) and §6.5 (one-SE rule). If the professor's statement there is stricter than what I've said — e.g. "never compare families by CV" — that is what goes in the memo and I'll defer to it.

Now I can answer from the professor's own text. Two things first: the brief's section numbers don't match these PDFs — what it calls "Handout 1 §6 / §6.5 / §7.2 / §7.3–7.4" is actually **Handout 2 §4 / §4.5 / §5.2 / §5.3–5.4** (`lecture02_evaluating_models.pdf`). Handout 1 stops at §5 and hands cross-validation off to Lecture 2. I'll record that mapping in `NOTES.md` at the end of the session.

## The re-answer: can CV choose the model family?

**Yes — the professor says so explicitly, in three places.**

1. **Handout 2 §3.1, "Three roles":** the validation set is "used to choose among *classes* or settings (the degree *d*, the number of trees, the penalty λ). These are called hyperparameters or tuning parameters." Choosing among classes is listed as the validation set's job, right alongside tuning.

2. **Handout 2, Exercise 1 and its solution:** a colleague compares a linear model (training MSE 4.1) with a random forest (training MSE 0.6). What single number settles which is better? "A cross-validated or held-out error for both, computed identically." That is choosing between OLS and a forest by CV, recommended by the professor.

3. **Handout 2 §6, the protocol:** step 3 is "run K-fold cross-validation *of the entire pipeline* to choose hyperparameters", step 4 the one-SE rule, step 5 refit, step 6 "evaluate once on the test set."

So what were you remembering? Almost certainly one of these two lines, which are real and are stricter than they sound:

- **Handout 1 §5.3:** empirical risk is unbiased "if the *m* cases are independent draws from *P* and *g* is fixed *before* we see them ... Read that condition again — *g fixed before we see the data* — because everything that follows turns on it." This is about which number you are allowed to *report*, not about which choices you are allowed to *make*. Any predictor you selected by looking at a score no longer has an unbiased score on that data. That applies equally to a coefficient chosen by ERM, a penalty chosen by CV, or a family chosen by CV — it is the same selection effect "applied one level up" (Handout 2 §3.1). The remedy is not to avoid selecting; it is to keep a test set that selected nothing.

- **Handout 2 §6, step 1:** "Decide what the *outcome* is, and what the *loss* is, before looking at any results." That is the thing genuinely fixed in advance: what you are predicting and what a mistake costs (Handout 1 §4.4, "the loss is a business statement"). Not the family.

And one honest caveat the professor attaches, which is probably the flavour you remembered: "The more candidates you compare, the worse it gets" (§3.1) and "CV is usually poor at identifying the exact minimizing model and quite good at identifying a model whose risk is near the minimum" (§4.4). Comparing three families is fine; comparing 300 recipes and reporting the winner's CV score is the mistake.

**What this means for the memo.** Both parts fix the class up front anyway — logistic regression in A, the four named specifications in B — so the question doesn't bite. Where it does bite is B2 question 2: comparing degree-1 vs degree-2 ridge across divisions *is* choosing a class by CV, and the professor's rule for doing it is §4.5, the one-SE rule: "select the simplest model whose CV error is within one standard error of the minimum," because "models inside that band are statistically indistinguishable." That is what the rubric means by "the single-standard question answered against penalised fits, with the one-standard-error rule used."

## Two things in this handout you should look at before A1/A2

- **Exercise 7 (Leakage triage)** and its solution are almost the A2 column decision. Item (f), a survey "sent to churn-risk accounts," is `winback_offer_sent` in different clothes; item (c), refunds "issued as part of the cancellation process," is `exit_survey_started`; item (a), `days_since_last_login`, is declared safe "if computed as of the prediction date." Handout 2 §5.2 gives the test: *at the exact moment I need this prediction, would this value be known, and would it have this value?*
- **Exercise 10 (Baselines)** is A1 verbatim: the three baselines before endorsing a 0.71 AUC are a single-variable ranking, the incumbent rule, and the model with suspected-leaky features removed. Plus: "AUC is not a business metric. Convert to expected cost." That is A3.

Want me to go on to Handout R1 (accuracy, AUC, the threshold formula) before we make the A1 calls, or are you ready to decide the A1 design now?

Now I can give you the professor's versions. Section numbers below are from the PDFs you just added.

## 1. Which "risk"?

The **true risk** (Handout 1 §5.1): R(f̂) = E[L(Y, f̂(X))], the expected loss on a fresh draw from the population. It is "the quantity we care about and can never compute." The sentence you quoted is reading Handout 2 Table 1, where the professor *can* compute it because the data are simulated: true risk is 0.334 at degree 5 (the minimum) and 0.354 at degree 7. CV picked degree 7 — the wrong degree — but a degree whose true risk is only 6% above the best. That's the claim: CV misses the exact minimiser and reliably lands in the flat region near it, and landing in the flat region is what matters.

## 2. The family of "risk" words

| Term | Definition | Where |
|---|---|---|
| **Risk** R(g) | Expected loss on a fresh draw from P. The target. Never computable. | H1 §5.1 |
| **Empirical risk** R̂_S(g) | Average loss on a sample S. Computable. | H1 §5.3 |
| **Training error** | Empirical risk when S is the training set. Biased *downward* because ERM chose g to fit those very points; the bias grows with flexibility. Never rises as the class grows (H2 Prop 2.1). | H1 §5.3, §5.5 |
| **Test error** | Empirical risk on cases that "played no role in producing g." Unbiased for R(g) *only because* g was fixed before those cases were seen. | H1 §5.3 |
| **Generalization gap** | R(f̂) − R̂_D(f̂). Small gap + low training error = good; low training error + big gap = **overfitting**; small gap + high training error = **underfitting**. | H1 §5.6 |
| **Irreducible error** Var(ε \| X) | The floor. Two customers identical on every recorded field, one churns, one doesn't. No model touches it. Test MSE ≥ σ² always; a number below it is contamination (H2 Ex. 2). | H1 §4.3 eq. (2); Part B's σ̂² |
| **Bayes error rate** | The irreducible floor for 0–1 loss: the risk of the best possible classifier. | H1 §5.2 |
| **CV estimate** CV_(K) | Average held-out loss over K rotating folds. An estimate of R for the *procedure*, slightly biased upward (each fit uses (K−1)/K of the data), with SE = sd(fold errors)/√K. | H2 §4.2, §4.5 |

## 3. Classification metrics (Handout R1 §4)

The professor's organising rule (R1 §1): **"Report a performance number as a distance from something"** — a trivial baseline, an irreducible floor, or the rule currently in use. "A number with no denominator invites the question 'is that good?' and cannot answer it." That is A1 in one sentence.

Three groups of metric, and the assignment uses one from each:

**Decision metrics at a fixed threshold** — from the confusion matrix (R1 §4.1):

| | ŷ = 1 | ŷ = 0 |
|---|---|---|
| y = 1 (churner) | TP | FN (miss) |
| y = 0 | FP (false alarm) | TN |

- **Accuracy** = (TP + TN) / all. "Only informative when the classes are roughly balanced *and* the two errors cost the same" (§4.2). At a 9% base rate, "nobody churns" scores 91% with zero information. That is the A1 sentence on why accuracy is wrong here — and there's a second reason in our case: the two errors don't cost the same ($18 vs $66).
- Precision = TP/(TP+FP), recall = TP/(TP+FN). Every one of these is a function of the threshold *t*; reporting one at *t* = 0.5 "reports one point on a curve you have not looked at" (§4.4).

**Ranking metric across all thresholds** — **AUC** (R1 §4.5, Prop. 4.1):

> Take one account that churned and one that did not, at random. AUC is the probability the model gave the churner the higher score. That is all it is.

So 0.5 = coin flip, 1.0 = perfect ordering. It is a statement about *ordering only*: it is blind to whether the probabilities are numerically right (a model whose probabilities are all 10× too big has the same AUC), it ignores your operating point, and it is "optimistic under severe imbalance." This is why a single column with no model — sort by `days_since_last_login` — has an AUC at all: any score that orders members has one. That is the A1 single-variable baseline.

**Metrics on the probabilities themselves** — log-loss, Brier, **calibration** (§4.7): a model is calibrated if among members scored p ≈ 0.2, about 20% churn. Ranking can be perfect while calibration is off, and "calibration matters the moment a probability is multiplied by money." It matters for A3, because the threshold below is applied to p as if it were a real probability.

## 4. The threshold (R1 §3, eq. 4)

"The threshold is not 0.5, and it is not a modelling decision." General form: if a false positive costs C_FP and a false negative costs C_FN, then for a case with probability p,

- predict 1 (act): expected cost (1 − p)·C_FP
- predict 0 (don't): expected cost p·C_FN

Act when (1 − p)·C_FP < p·C_FN, i.e. **p > t\* = C_FP / (C_FP + C_FN)**. Only the *ratio* of costs matters.

For Northline the brief gives it in gains rather than costs, and A3 wants you to derive it from those, so here is the same argument in the brief's terms:

- offer to a would-be churner: +$66 (= 240 × 0.35 − 18)
- offer to a non-churner: −$18
- no offer: $0

Expected value of offering to a member with churn probability p: 66p − 18(1 − p) = 84p − 18. That beats $0 when p > 18/84 ≈ **0.214**. Same answer as eq. (4) with C_FP = 18 (the wasted offer) and C_FN = 66 (the forgone gain from a missed churner): 18/(18 + 66). Note the analyst's script instead *searches* a grid of thresholds for the one that maximises realised value ([analyst_model.py:90-92](part1_northline/starter/analyst_model.py#L90-L92)) — that is picking the threshold by looking at the outcome, which the professor calls "selection on the outcome" (R1 §4.9). The memo should derive it from the finance table, and you'll want to write that derivation out yourself for the midterm.

The R1 D12 example (§3) shows why it matters: the same model at t = 0.5 captured 17% of its available value; at the cost-derived t = 0.048 it captured nearly all of it. "Nothing about the model changed between those two rows."

for the generalization gap, what is the first and second types of risks there that we are subtracting?
for CV estimate, does it try to approximate the real risk (R - Rxpected risk?) or is it approxiamting empriical risk

==============================================================================
ASSISTANT  [2026-09-17T21:13:37.639Z]
==============================================================================

**Generalization gap: R(f̂) − R̂_D(f̂)**

- **First term, R(f̂):** the *true risk* of the fitted predictor — its expected loss on a fresh draw from the population. Unknowable.
- **Second term, R̂_D(f̂):** the *empirical risk on the training set D* — the training error. Known.

Same predictor f̂, two different data sources: the whole population versus the sample it was fitted on. The gap is "the amount by which true risk exceeds what the training data reported" (H1 §5.6). It's positive in expectation because ERM picked f̂ to look good on D specifically. Note the professor's framing: the gap measures **honesty**, not quality — a constant predictor has a gap of ~0 and is useless; a model with a gap of 0.30 might still have the lower risk (H1 Exercise 10).

**CV: what is it approximating?**

The true risk R — expected loss on fresh draws. Not the empirical risk (which you can just compute; nothing needs to *approximate* it).

Every term in CV_(K) is a held-out loss: fold k is scored by a model that never saw it, so for that model, fold k *is* a fresh draw from P. That's why each MSE_k is an honest empirical-risk estimate of that model's true risk (H1 §5.3's condition — "g fixed before we see the data" — holds fold by fold). Averaging the K of them gives the CV estimate.

One precision, which the professor is careful about in H2 §4.3: CV estimates the risk of the *procedure* (fit this recipe on ~(K−1)/K·n rows), not of the final refitted model. Since the final model sees all n rows and more data helps, the CV number is slightly pessimistic for the model you deploy — the "upward bias" in the §4.3 table, which shrinks as K grows. So: CV ≈ R, from slightly above, for a model fitted on a bit less data than the one you ship.

==============================================================================

Fair — let me untangle the vocabulary first, because the "procedure" point falls out of it.

## Three words, three levels

| Word | Applies to | One sentence |
|---|---|---|
| **Loss** L(y, ŷ) | *one case* | What one mistake costs. Squared error (y − ŷ)², or $18/$66 for a churn offer. A number per row. |
| **Risk** R(g) | *one predictor g, whole population* | The average loss g would incur over every case the world could throw at it. E[L(Y, g(X))]. Unknowable, because the population is. |
| **Error** (training error, test error, CV error, fold error) | *one predictor g, one named sample* | The average loss over the rows of a specific sample. This is *empirical risk* R̂_S(g); "error" is the everyday word for it, and the adjective tells you which sample S. |

So: loss is the ingredient, risk is its population average, error is its sample average. An "error" is always an estimate of a risk; the question is whether it's an honest one. Training error is a biased estimate of R(f̂) because f̂ was chosen to fit that sample. Test error is an honest one because f̂ was fixed before the test rows were seen (H1 §5.3).

## Now the "procedure" point

Risk is a property of *one specific predictor*. Watch what CV actually has in hand:

- Round 1 fits f̂₍₋₁₎ and computes fold-1 error. That is an honest estimate of **R(f̂₍₋₁₎)** — the risk of *that* model.
- Round 2 fits a *different* model f̂₍₋₂₎ and computes fold-2 error → honest estimate of **R(f̂₍₋₂₎)**.
- … five models, five risks, five estimates.

CV averages them. The average of five different models' risks isn't the risk of any one model — certainly not of the final model, which is a sixth one fitted on all the data and never scored. What it *is* an estimate of is: "if I run this recipe on a training sample of this size, what true risk does the model I get typically have?" That is what "risk of the procedure" means. Still the true risk, still the population expectation, but averaged over which training sample the recipe happened to receive.

You can see why this is the right target: you want to know whether the *recipe* — these columns, this model class, this penalty — is worth deploying, before you've committed to a final fit. The final model's own risk then gets measured once, on the test set.

The one caveat carried from before: the five CV models each saw 4/5 of the data, the final model sees all of it, so CV slightly overstates the final model's risk. Small, and in the safe direction.

**Short version to hold onto:** *Loss* is per case. *Risk* is the population average for a fixed predictor, never computable. *Error* is the sample average, always computable, honest only if the sample played no part in fitting. CV gives an honest error for each fold's model and averages them to estimate what risk this recipe produces.

==============================================================================
ASSISTANT  [2026-09-17T21:21:15.814Z]
==============================================================================

**Yes.** R(g) is the risk *of g*, and g is one completely specified function — model type *and* the fitted coefficients (and, if g outputs a decision, the threshold too). Change a coefficient and you have a different g with a different risk. That is why H1 §4.2 says "f̂ depends on D, so f̂ is itself random": a different training sample → different coefficients → different predictor → different risk.

## The loss, with the Northline dollars

A loss function takes two things — **what actually happened** (y) and **what you did** (ŷ) — and returns what that combination cost you, with L(y, y) = 0 (H1 §4.4). It is defined per case: one member, one month, one number.

For Northline the outcome is y ∈ {churns, stays} and the action is ŷ ∈ {offer, no offer}. The brief gives you *values* for each combination; to make them a loss you measure each cell against the best thing you could have done had you known y (this is called regret):

| Truth y | Action ŷ | Value (brief) | Best possible for this y | **Loss** |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| churns | offer | +$66 | +$66 | **0** |
| churns | no offer | $0 | +$66 | **$66** — a *miss* (false negative) |
| stays | offer | −$18 | $0 | **$18** — a *false alarm* (false positive) |
| stays | no offer | $0 | $0 | **0** |

That is exactly H1 §4.6's cost-weighted loss, L = C_FN·𝟙{churns, no offer} + C_FP·𝟙{stays, offer}, with C_FN = 66 and C_FP = 18. Two of the four cells cost nothing; the other two cost different amounts — which is the whole reason accuracy (0–1 loss, every mistake costs 1) is the wrong loss here.

Now stack the three levels on this one loss:

- **Loss** — one member: you offered, they were going to stay anyway, loss $18.
- **Risk of a decision rule g** — the average loss per member if you ran g across the whole April 2026 book. Unknowable.
- **Test error of g** — the average loss over the 2025-10 → 12 rows, which is what A3's "realised dollar value of the program on the test period" is, in gain form instead of loss form (they differ by a constant, so the ranking of rules is identical).

## Where the threshold comes from

The model gives p, not a decision. The loss is defined on decisions. So for a member with churn probability p you compute the expected loss of each action:

- offer: you only lose if they stay → (1 − p) × 18
- no offer: you only lose if they churn → p × 66

Offer when (1 − p)·18 < p·66, i.e. p > 18/84 ≈ 0.214. That's R1 eq. (4) again, and it's where the loss table and the model meet: the threshold is the rule that minimises expected loss at each p. Quick sanity check at p = 0.09 (the base rate): offering costs 0.91 × 18 ≈ $16.4 expected, not offering costs 0.09 × 66 ≈ $5.9 — so a member about whom you know nothing should *not* get an offer. That's why "offer everyone" is a losing program and why A3 asks you to compare against it.

ASSISTANT  [2026-09-17T21:24:06.088Z]
==============================================================================

Sharp question, and the answer is no — for two reasons, one about time and one about what "population" means in the handout.

**Time.** At the moment you have to decide (March 2026, when the VP plans), the April book doesn't exist yet, and its outcomes — who cancels within 60 days — don't exist until the end of June. You can't average over what hasn't happened. By the time you *can* compute it, the decision it was supposed to inform is nine months old.

**What P is.** Handout 1 §4.1 defines the population not as a list of people but as "the process that generates our customers" — a joint probability distribution over (X, Y). The April book is one *draw* from that process. So is May's. And crucially, even for a fixed member, the outcome is random: Y = f(X) + ε (§4.3), and two members identical on every recorded field can go different ways. So the April book's realised loss is one sample of the risk, wobbling around it by however much the ε's happened to fall that quarter. Risk is the expectation; April's number is one observation of it.

What you *can* compute, after June 2026, is the **realised error on the April book** — empirical risk on a sample that played no role in fitting. That is exactly what H2 §6 step 8 means by "after launch, monitor the same metric on live data. It will differ from your test number, and the size of that difference is the most useful thing you will learn all quarter." It's an honest error, and a very good one, but it's still an error, not the risk.

So the hierarchy holds even here: the risk is the target you aim at with the test error now and the live error later. Neither one *is* it — they're both estimates, the second one being much closer to the deployment conditions than the first.

ASSISTANT  [2026-09-17T21:26:24.006Z]

==============================================================================
ASSISTANT  [2026-09-17T21:26:33.002Z]
==============================================================================

Close, with two corrections that matter for the exam:

1. **True risk is not an estimate.** It's the *target* — a fixed number that exists but you can't see. Empirical risk is the estimate of it. "Both are estimates" would be wrong; one is the thing, the other is the measurement.

2. **"True empirical risk" isn't a phrase.** Empirical risk is always fully computable the moment you have a sample *with outcomes*: add up the losses, divide by n. There's no "true" version of it lurking behind. The only thing that changes over time is *which samples you have outcomes for* — the training set now, the test window now, the April book after June 2026.

And on *why* true risk can never be computed: noise is part of it but not the whole of it. Risk is an expectation over the distribution P of (X, Y), and P is unknown on both coordinates — you don't know the distribution of future members (which mix of corporate vs. organic, how the competitor changes behaviour), and you don't know the outcome given the member (the ε). Even in a world with zero noise you still couldn't compute R exactly, because you'd still be averaging over customers who haven't arrived yet.

So, cleanly:

- **Risk** R(g): the population average loss. A fixed, unknown number. The target.
- **Empirical risk** R̂_S(g): the sample average loss on S. Always computable once S has outcomes. An estimate of R — honest if g was fixed before S was seen, biased if S was used to fit g.

Every "error" in the course (training, test, CV, fold, live) is an empirical risk on some named sample, and the entire evaluation design question is: *which sample makes it an honest estimate of the risk you'll face at deployment?*

when you say (the ε) do you mean estimation or noise epsilon

==============================================================================
ASSISTANT  [2026-09-17T21:27:44.985Z]
==============================================================================

Noise. In Handout 1 §4.3, Y = f(X) + ε, and ε is "everything else: measurement error, omitted variables, and genuine randomness" — the part of the outcome that the recorded inputs don't determine. Two members identical on every column, one churns, one stays: the difference is ε. Its variance, Var(ε | X), is the irreducible error, the floor in equation (2), and it's what Part B estimates as σ̂² from the replicates.

There's no separate "estimation epsilon" in the handouts. The estimation side of things — how far your fitted f̂ is from the true f — shows up as the *reducible* term in eq. (2), (f(x₀) − f̂(x₀))², and Handout 3 will split that further into bias and variance. Different symbol, different concept: ε is about the world, the reducible term is about your model.

ASSISTANT  [2026-09-17T21:28:34.573Z]
==============================================================================

Almost — and the way it falls short is instructive.

Even at the end of time you'd have a finite pile of cases, and the average loss over a pile is still an *empirical* risk: a huge sample, negligible sampling error, but a sample. Risk isn't "the average over everyone who ever showed up"; it's the expectation under the *process*. Think of a coin: the probability of heads is 0.5 as a property of the coin, not "the fraction of heads once all flipping stops." Every flip you ever observe is a draw from it.

There's a second reason the end-of-time average wouldn't be *the* risk anyway: P isn't constant. Handout 1 §2.3 calls this distribution shift — the competitor launching, corporate going from 0% to 35%. The risk you care about in A3 is the risk under the *April 2026* process. All customers ever would be a blend of many different P's, none of them the one you're planning against.

So the accurate version is: risk is a property of the process at a moment, like a probability; every sample of outcomes, however large, is an estimate of it.

Exactly. "Empirical" literally means "from observation" — so anything computed from a sample of observed cases is empirical risk by definition, no matter how large the sample. Risk is defined as an expectation under P, and P is a probability distribution, not a dataset. You can only ever estimate an expectation from draws; you can't observe it directly. That's the whole reason Handout 1 §5.4 calls ERM "the substitution at the heart of machine learning": we want to minimise R, we can't, so we minimise R̂_D and then spend the rest of the course managing the consequences.
