Good. First decision: **what the base rate is computed on.** Two sub-choices bundled here because they're about the same thing — which rows count.

## What we're computing and why

The base rate is P(churn_next_60d = 1), the no-information answer. It's the denominator for everything after it: the accuracy of "nobody churns" is 1 − base rate, the precision of a random mailing is the base rate, and the threshold check I showed earlier (at p = 0.09, don't offer) uses it. Expectation before computing: ~9%, per the brief.

## Sub-choice A — duplicates

The brief mandates removing NLF-2231 duplicates ("every affected row appears twice, identical on every column"). Not a design choice, but it affects the base rate slightly and it affects the *comparison* with the analyst, who did not dedupe. I'd:

- `drop_duplicates()` on all columns, after normalising `snapshot_month` with `month_key()` — otherwise two copies of the same row with the date in two formats wouldn't match. Actually the ticket says the re-run appended identical rows, so format should match, but normalising first is the safe order and costs nothing.
- Report rows before and after, and the number removed, so the number is auditable.
- Compute A1 on the deduped rows, and say so in the table. The analyst's 0.967 sits beside it as-computed (raw rows), labelled as such.

One thing I want to verify in code rather than assume: that the duplicates are *only* in March 2025 and that after dedup every (member_id, month) pair is unique. If a member still has two rows for one month after dedup, they're not identical duplicates and we'd need to look.

## Sub-choice B — the unit

| Option | What it is | Implication |
|---|---|---|
| **(a) Row-level** | share of member-months with label 1 | Matches the analyst's unit, the brief's "9% in any 60-day window", and how the program scores the book (monthly). The headline. |
| **(b) Member-level** | share of members who ever churn in the window | Higher number; answers "what fraction of the customer base leaves eventually." Not what the program acts on. |
| **(c) Both** | (a) as the headline, (b) as one line of context | Costs one line; lets you say out loud why they differ if asked. |

My recommendation is **(c)** with (a) as the number that goes in the exhibit table — but that's your call. Also worth deciding: do you want the base rate additionally broken out by year (2024 vs 2025)? It isn't asked in A1, but it's a cheap check that the world drifted (competitor from July 2025), and it feeds your A1 paragraph on "what would have to be true for 0.967 to hold on the April 2026 book." I'd lean yes, as a small diagnostic table separate from the A1 exhibit.

Your calls: A as described (yes/no), B (a, b, or c), and the by-year breakout (yes/no).

what is the by year breakout, also are we expected to clean the data before we answer A1 or not

ASSISTANT  [2026-09-17T21:42:18.864Z]

**By-year breakout** — just the base rate computed separately for 2024 rows and 2025 rows (or per month, which is the same idea at finer grain). One extra `groupby`. The point: the brief says a competitor launched July 2025 and corporate members ramped through 2025. If the churn rate in late 2025 differs noticeably from 2024, that's direct evidence the data-generating process moved — which is the strongest argument you can make in the A1 paragraph about why a number pooled over all 24 months isn't an estimate for April 2026. It's a diagnostic to inform your paragraph, not an A1 deliverable, and it can be dropped if you'd rather keep A1 minimal.

**Cleaning before A1** — the brief is explicit for the duplicates: "Remove the duplicates before modelling," and A1's own heading is "before any modelling." It doesn't spell out whether the baselines are pre- or post-dedup, but the base rate is a fact about the population, and the duplicated March 2025 rows are one month counted twice, which distorts that fact. So I'd say yes, dedup first, for A1 and everything after. The two cleaning steps are:

1. **Normalise `snapshot_month`** with `month_key()` — three date formats into one. This isn't optional at any stage; nothing time-based works without it.
2. **Drop exact duplicate rows** — the NLF-2231 re-run.

Nothing else. No column dropping (that's A2 change 1, and A1 explicitly comes before it), no filtering of members or months, no imputation. The A1 "rule" for the single-variable AUC uses a column as-is.

One nuance to be honest about in the exhibit: the analyst's 0.967 was computed on the *raw* file — duplicates in. If you list your numbers on deduped rows beside theirs on raw rows, say so. Either is defensible; hiding it isn't.

So: dedup for A1, yes. Do you want the by-year breakout kept as a small diagnostic, and which base-rate unit — (a) row-level only, or (c) row-level headline with the member-level figure as one line of context?

ASSISTANT  [2026-09-17T21:50:51.713Z]
==============================================================================

**"Headline"** — journalism metaphor. The headline is the one number a report leads with and that everyone repeats: here, "0.967 AUC." The brief uses it that way ("the analyst's headline AUC") and so does Handout R1 ("if a report leads with accuracy on an imbalanced problem…"). The connotation is slightly suspicious: a headline is what you read without reading the article, so the course's whole reflex is "what's underneath it?"

**A single-variable AUC.** Remember what AUC is (R1 Prop. 4.1): pick one churner and one non-churner at random; AUC is the probability the *score* ranks the churner higher. It needs only a score that orders members. Nothing says the score has to come from a model.

So take a raw column and use it as the score. For `days_since_last_login`, the "rule" is: *the longer since you last logged in, the more likely you are to churn*. Sort every member-month by that column, descending. Then AUC answers: for a random churner and a random non-churner, how often does the churner have the larger `days_since_last_login`? If login recency told you nothing, 0.5. If every churner had been away longer than every non-churner, 1.0.

Mechanically it's `roc_auc_score(y, df["days_since_last_login"])` — the same function the analyst calls, fed a column instead of a `predict_proba`. Two consequences of that:

- **No fitting, no CV.** There are no coefficients to estimate, so there's nothing that can overfit and no need for folds. You compute it on all deduped rows. The one "choice" is the direction: for `sessions_last_30d` the sensible rule is *fewer sessions → more churn*, so you'd score by the negative of the column (or equivalently note that an AUC below 0.5 means "flip it").
- **What it tells you.** It's the floor for a real model: if one column with no model gets, say, 0.70, then a model at 0.75 has added 0.05, not 0.75 (H2 Exercise 10). And it works as a leak detector: a column that alone scores near 1 isn't a great predictor, it's the outcome wearing a disguise.

That's why the brief's example is `days_since_last_login` — a behavioural column that is genuinely available at scoring time and should carry real but modest signal. Expectation: somewhere in 0.6–0.75.

The one remaining call there is whether to compute it for **just that column** or for **every admissible numeric column** and show them all in a small table (still one rule each, just a table of them). The brief asks for "one"; the rubric says "at least one." A table of several costs nothing and gives you a feel for where the signal lives before A2 — but it's a table you'd be looking at, and I'd want you to decide before seeing it that it's a diagnostic, not a feature-selection step.

Other questions, or ready for that call and the file layout?

Locking in the design before I write anything:

- **Clean:** `month_key()` normalise, then `drop_duplicates()` on all columns. Verify (member_id, month) is unique afterwards and report which months had duplicates.
- **Base rate:** row-level (headline, matches the analyst's unit) and member-level (context). Plus by-year and by-month breakouts as a diagnostic file.
- **Trivial rule:** predict 0 for everyone → accuracy.
- **Single-variable AUCs**, directions fixed now from domain logic, not flipped after the fact: `days_since_last_login` (higher → churn), `tenure_months` (lower → churn), `app_opens_last_30d` (lower → churn), `avg_session_minutes` (lower → churn).
- **Analyst headline:** run `analyst_model.py` unmodified once via `run.sh`, capture its output to `analysis/outputs/`, and the A1 script reads the AUC from there.
- **Layout:** `analysis/run.sh` → `analysis/part1/a1_baselines.py`, outputs in `analysis/outputs/`.

[tool_use: Write]
{
  "file_path": "/Users/orcuntasdemir/Desktop/a1_evaluation_and_flexibility/analysis/part1/a1_baselines.py",
  "content": "\"\"\"\nA1 -- baselines before any modelling.  Northline Fitness churn.\n\nWhat this produces, all written to analysis/outputs/:\n\n    a1_baselines.csv            the A1 exhibit: base rate, trivial-rule\n                                accuracy, single-variable AUCs, analyst's AUC\n    a1_base_rate_by_year.csv    diagnostic: does the base rate move over time?\n    a1_base_rate_by_month.csv   same, monthly\n    a1_cleaning.txt             what dedup removed, and the uniqueness check\n\nCleaning done here, and nothing else: normalise snapshot_month with the\nsupplied month_key(), then drop exact duplicate rows (ticket NLF-2231).  No\ncolumn is dropped -- that is A2 change 1, and A1 comes before it.\n\nSingle-variable rules need no fitting, so there is no cross-validation and\nno randomness in this script.  The direction of each rule is fixed below\nfrom what the column means, before the number is seen, and is not flipped\nafterwards.\n\"\"\"\nimport re\nimport sys\nfrom pathlib import Path\n\nimport numpy as np\nimport pandas as pd\nfrom sklearn.metrics import accuracy_score, roc_auc_score\n\nHERE = Path(__file__).resolve().parent\nROOT = HERE.parents[1]\nOUT = ROOT / \"analysis\" / \"outputs\"\n\n# Use the supplied loader rather than reimplementing it.\nsys.path.insert(0, str(ROOT / \"part1_northline\" / \"starter\"))\nfrom load import TARGET, load_raw, month_key  # noqa: E402\n\nANALYST_OUTPUT = OUT / \"analyst_model_output.txt\"\nANALYST_QUOTED_AUC = 0.967   # the figure in the brief, used only as a label\n\n# Single-variable rules.  sign=+1 means \"higher value -> more likely to\n# churn\"; sign=-1 means the opposite.  Chosen from the column definitions in\n# the data dictionary, not from the data.\nSINGLE_VARIABLE_RULES = {\n    \"days_since_last_login\": (+1, \"longer since last login -> more likely to churn\"),\n    \"tenure_months\":         (-1, \"shorter tenure -> more likely to churn\"),\n    \"app_opens_last_30d\":    (-1, \"fewer app opens -> more likely to churn\"),\n    \"avg_session_minutes\":   (-1, \"shorter sessions -> more likely to churn\"),\n}\n\n\ndef clean(df: pd.DataFrame) -> tuple[pd.DataFrame, list[str]]:\n    \"\"\"Normalise the month, drop exact duplicates, and record what happened.\"\"\"\n    notes = []\n    df = df.copy()\n    df[\"month\"] = df[\"snapshot_month\"].map(month_key)\n    df[\"year\"] = df[\"month\"].str[:4]\n\n    before = len(df)\n    dup_mask = df.duplicated(keep=\"first\")\n    dup_months = df.loc[dup_mask, \"month\"].value_counts().sort_index()\n    df = df.loc[~dup_mask].reset_index(drop=True)\n    notes.append(f\"rows as delivered           {before:,}\")\n    notes.append(f\"exact duplicate rows removed {int(dup_mask.sum()):,}\")\n    notes.append(f\"rows after dedup             {len(df):,}\")\n    notes.append(\"duplicates by month: \" +\n                 \", \".join(f\"{m}={n:,}\" for m, n in dup_months.items()))\n\n    # After removing exact duplicates, one row per member per month is what\n    # the dictionary promises.  Check it rather than assume it.\n    remaining = df.duplicated(subset=[\"member_id\", \"month\"]).sum()\n    notes.append(f\"(member_id, month) pairs still duplicated after dedup: {remaining:,}\")\n    if remaining:\n        raise RuntimeError(\"non-identical duplicate member-months remain; inspect before continuing\")\n\n    notes.append(f\"unique members               {df['member_id'].nunique():,}\")\n    notes.append(f\"months covered               {df['month'].min()} -> {df['month'].max()} \"\n                 f\"({df['month'].nunique()} months)\")\n    return df, notes\n\n\ndef analyst_auc() -> float | None:\n    \"\"\"Read the AUC the analyst's script printed, if run.sh has captured it.\"\"\"\n    if not ANALYST_OUTPUT.exists():\n        return None\n    m = re.search(r\"cross-validated AUC\\s+([0-9.]+)\", ANALYST_OUTPUT.read_text())\n    return float(m.group(1)) if m else None\n\n\ndef main() -> None:\n    OUT.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)\n    df, notes = clean(load_raw())\n    (OUT / \"a1_cleaning.txt\").write_text(\"\\n\".join(notes) + \"\\n\")\n    print(\"\\n\".join(notes))\n    print()\n\n    y = df[TARGET].to_numpy()\n    n_rows, n_pos = len(y), int(y.sum())\n\n    # 1. Base rate.  Row-level is the unit the analyst's model and the monthly\n    #    programme use.  Member-level is context: a member who ever churns.\n    base_rate_rows = y.mean()\n    base_rate_members = df.groupby(\"member_id\")[TARGET].max().mean()\n\n    # 2. The trivial rule: predict \"no churn\" for everyone.\n    trivial_acc = accuracy_score(y, np.zeros_like(y))\n\n    # 3. Single-variable rules, scored on all deduped rows.  No fitting.\n    rule_rows = []\n    for col, (sign, meaning) in SINGLE_VARIABLE_RULES.items():\n        auc = roc_auc_score(y, sign * df[col].to_numpy())\n        rule_rows.append((col, sign, meaning, auc))\n\n    # 4. The analyst's headline, as their script printed it.\n    a_auc = analyst_auc()\n\n    table = [\n        {\"quantity\": \"base rate of churn_next_60d (rows)\",\n         \"value\": round(base_rate_rows, 4),\n         \"n\": n_rows, \"positives\": n_pos,\n         \"note\": \"deduped member-months; unit matches the analyst's model\"},\n        {\"quantity\": \"base rate (members ever churning)\",\n         \"value\": round(base_rate_members, 4),\n         \"n\": df[\"member_id\"].nunique(),\n         \"positives\": int(df.groupby(\"member_id\")[TARGET].max().sum()),\n         \"note\": \"context only; different unit\"},\n        {\"quantity\": \"accuracy of predicting 'no churn' for everyone\",\n         \"value\": round(trivial_acc, 4), \"n\": n_rows, \"positives\": n_pos,\n         \"note\": \"= 1 - base rate; no information used\"},\n    ]\n    for col, sign, meaning, auc in rule_rows:\n        table.append({\"quantity\": f\"AUC, single-variable rule: {col}\",\n                      \"value\": round(auc, 4), \"n\": n_rows, \"positives\": n_pos,\n                      \"note\": f\"{meaning}; all deduped rows, no fitting\"})\n    table.append({\"quantity\": \"analyst's 5-fold CV AUC (all columns, raw rows)\",\n                  \"value\": a_auc if a_auc is not None else \"not run\",\n                  \"n\": \"33,340 raw rows\", \"positives\": \"\",\n                  \"note\": f\"as printed by starter/analyst_model.py; brief quotes {ANALYST_QUOTED_AUC}\"})\n    exhibit = pd.DataFrame(table)\n    exhibit.to_csv(OUT / \"a1_baselines.csv\", index=False)\n\n    # Diagnostic: base rate over time.  Not an A1 deliverable; evidence for the\n    # paragraph on whether a pooled 2024-2025 number speaks to April 2026.\n    by_year = (df.groupby(\"year\")[TARGET]\n               .agg(rows=\"size\", churners=\"sum\", base_rate=\"mean\").reset_index())\n    by_month = (df.groupby(\"month\")[TARGET]\n                .agg(rows=\"size\", churners=\"sum\", base_rate=\"mean\").reset_index())\n    corp_share = (df.assign(corp=df[\"acquisition_channel\"].eq(\"corporate\"))\n                  .groupby(\"year\")[\"corp\"].mean().rename(\"corporate_share\"))\n    by_year = by_year.merge(corp_share, on=\"year\")\n    by_year.to_csv(OUT / \"a1_base_rate_by_year.csv\", index=False)\n    by_month.to_csv(OUT / \"a1_base_rate_by_month.csv\", index=False)\n\n    print(\"A1 exhibit\")\n    print(exhibit.to_string(index=False))\n    print()\n    print(\"base rate by year (diagnostic)\")\n    print(by_year.to_string(index=False, float_format=lambda v: f\"{v:.4f}\"))\n    print()\n    print(\"base rate by month, last six (diagnostic)\")\n    print(by_month.tail(6).to_string(index=False, float_format=lambda v: f\"{v:.4f}\"))\n    print()\n    print(f\"wrote {OUT / 'a1_baselines.csv'}, a1_base_rate_by_year.csv, \"\n          f\"a1_base_rate_by_month.csv, a1_cleaning.txt\")\n\n\nif __name__ == \"__main__\":\n    main()\n"
}

  "file_path": "/Users/orcuntasdemir/Desktop/a1_evaluation_and_flexibility/analysis/README.md",
  "content": "# Analysis — how to run\n\nOne command, from the assignment folder root:\n\n    bash analysis/run.sh\n\nRequires Python 3.10+ with numpy, pandas, scikit-learn and matplotlib. No\nother packages. No GPU. Pinned versions used to produce the submitted numbers\nare in `../requirements.txt` (Python 3.11; numpy 2.4.6, pandas 3.0.5,\nscikit-learn 1.9.1, matplotlib 3.11.2). ## Exhibit A1 — Baselines (draft)

| Quantity | Value | Basis |
|---|---|---:|
| Base rate of `churn_next_60d` | **9.29%** (2,939 of 31,645 member-months) | deduplicated rows, Jan 2024 – Dec 2025 |
| *Context:* members who churn at some point in the window | 70.0% (2,939 of 4,200) | member level — a different unit |
| Accuracy of predicting "no churn" for everyone | **90.71%** | = 1 − base rate; uses no information |
| AUC, rank by `avg_session_minutes` (shorter → churn) | **0.681** | one column, no fitting, all rows |
| AUC, rank by `app_opens_last_30d` (fewer → churn) | 0.648 | " |
| AUC, rank by `days_since_last_login` (longer → churn) | 0.646 | " |
| AUC, rank by `tenure_months` (shorter → churn) | 0.502 | " |
| **Analyst's 5-fold CV AUC, all 14 columns** | **0.967** | raw 33,340 rows, random shuffled folds |

**Accuracy sentence (draft).** 90.7% is what you get by doing nothing, so it cannot be the yardstick; and it counts a missed churner ($66 forgone) and a wasted offer ($18) as the same mistake, which the finance table says they are not.

**Paragraph on what would have to be true for 0.967 (draft).** For 0.967 to describe April 2026, four things would have to hold. Every column the model uses would have to exist at the moment of scoring — but `winback_offer_sent` and `exit_survey_started` are recorded after month close or at cancellation, and the label sits on each churner's final row, exactly where those columns get populated. The April book would have to look like the pooled 2024–25 file — but corporate members were 0% of 2024, 10% of 2025 rows, and are forecast at 35%. The churn process would have to be stable — but the monthly rate ranged from 5.3% to 14.2%, with the highest two months at the competitor's July 2025 launch. And predicting a member's next month from that member's own other months would have to count as prediction — which is what random folds allow. Against that, the best single column reaches 0.68; a linear combination of fourteen columns lifting that to 0.97 is the pattern Handout 2 §5.2 describes: "a model that suddenly performs implausibly well has almost always found a leak."

## Arguing against it

- **"The 0.68 → 0.97 gap proves leakage."** It doesn't. Fourteen columns can legitimately beat any one; that's what a model is for. The gap is a *suspicion*, testable in A2 change 1. Write it as suspicion until the test is run.
- **"The competitor caused the Jul–Aug 2025 spike."** Look at 2024: Jul–Sep also peaked at 12.4–13.3%. With two years you can't separate seasonality from the launch. Say "coincides with," not "caused by."
- **`tenure_months` at 0.502.** Not a bug. A single monotone rule can't capture "new members and very old members churn differently." It also warns that a linear model in tenure would miss the same thing.
- **Row-level vs member-level.** 70% of members churning over 24 months sounds alarming beside 9%, but it's consistent: a ~4.5%/month hazard compounds to ~67% over 24 months. Both numbers are right; only the row-level one matches the analyst's unit and the program's monthly scoring.
- **Deduped vs raw.** Your rows are 31,645 and the analyst's 33,340. The 1,695 duplicates are all March 2025 (base rate 6.0%, below average), so deduping *raises* the base rate slightly. The two units line up otherwise; say so in a footnote rather than let a grader notice the row counts differ.

## Two things I'd flag

1. **Your expectation was met, which is the moment to slow down.** Base rate ≈ 9%, single-variable AUCs 0.65–0.68, all as predicted. Nothing errored. Course doc §4: "if the first answer confirms what you expected, slow down rather than speed up." The one number that *isn't* boring is the label structure — one flag per churner, always the last row — and it isn't in the brief.
2. **The book shrinks through the test window** (2,119 → 1,376 rows). Not an A1 item, but the A3 dollar figure is a sum over rows in Oct–Dec 2025, so it will be on a smaller base than the analyst's "per quarter" — worth remembering when you compare.

Now in the run output. (The `cut` mangled the quoted rows; the CSV itself is fine.)

## 3. If not accuracy, what? Not precision or recall either — the loss

The professor's answer is in R1 §4.9's table: for "rare positives, expensive misses" report "recall at a fixed precision, *plus the cost table of §3*." And H2 Exercise 10: "AUC is not a business metric. Convert to expected cost using the loss from Lecture 1 and the actual intervention cost."

So the metric for *this decision* is the **expected cost (or value) under the $18/$66 loss** — which is exactly A3's "realised dollar value of the program at the threshold." Precision and recall are ingredients of that number, not substitutes for it: at the threshold, precision = share of offers that hit a churner (each miss costs $18), recall = share of churners you reached (each one missed costs $66). Reporting either alone is meaningless ("recall of 1.0 is free if you flag everybody," R1 §4.3), and the dollar figure is what combines them with the right weights.

AUC has a different, narrower job: comparing rankings *before a threshold exists* (R1 §4.9, row 3). That's why A2 uses it — we're comparing evaluation protocols, not deciding who gets an offer. A useful sentence for the memo: "AUC is used to compare protocols; dollars at the cost-derived threshold are used to decide." One more thing to be aware of: with 9% positives, R1 §4.6 says the precision–recall curve and its area (AP) are the more honest ranking summary — the analyst printed AP 0.899 too. The brief asks for AUC, so use AUC, but you now know why someone might ask.

## 4. The last-row fact — you're right, and I overstated it

Obviously a churner's cancellation month is their last month. That part is trivial. What I should have said is narrower, and it's two things:

**(a) The label is not what the dictionary says.** The dictionary defines `churn_next_60d` as "terminated within 60 days of the end of `snapshot_month`." Take a member who cancels on 10 May. Sixty days back from that covers the end of April *and* the end of March — so a literal 60-day window should flag **two** rows for most churners. The extract flags exactly **one**, always the last. So in practice the label means "this is the member's final month on the book," a ~30-day window, not 60. That's a discrepancy between documentation and data, worth one line in the memo, and it's the kind of thing you'd want to be able to say if asked "what does a 1 actually mean in your target?"

**(b) It makes the leakage mechanism concrete rather than hypothetical.** The row being scored *is* the cancellation month. `exit_survey_started` is "1 if the member opened the cancellation exit survey" — that happens during the act of cancelling, i.e., during that same month. At the start of the month, when the April 2026 model has to score, it hasn't happened yet. Same for `winback_offer_sent`, populated from a list circulated after month close. So those columns aren't "correlated with churn"; they are recorded *by the process of churning*, on the row that carries the label. That's H2 §5.2's "target leakage: a field populated as a consequence of the outcome," and it's why 0.967 looks the way it does. Your instinct — "obviously that's the last row" — is exactly the argument: it's so obvious that it's easy to miss that the analyst's model is being credited for it.

So: not interesting as a discovery, useful as the sentence that turns "I suspect leakage" into "here is the mechanism." Keep going, or do you want to look at the exhibit draft again?

ASSISTANT  [2026-09-17T22:48:47.839Z]

They are **not** all zero, and the model doesn't need a "next month" — the 1 sits on the very row that carries the label. Here's what the extract shows:

| Column | Rows with 1 | Of those, churners | P(churn \| =1) | P(churn \| =0) | AUC alone |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| `exit_survey_started` | 1,810 (5.7%) | **1,810 — every one** | **1.000** | 0.038 | 0.808 |
| `winback_offer_sent` | 3,533 (11.2%) | 2,239 | 0.634 | 0.025 | 0.858 |

And every one of the 1,810 `exit_survey_started = 1` rows is a member's last row.

So the picture is:

- **`exit_survey_started`.** Opening the exit survey is a step in cancelling. If it's 1, the member churned, with zero exceptions in 31,645 rows. It covers 62% of churners (1,810 of 2,939). A single yes/no column with no false positives that catches 62% of the positives gets AUC 0.81 by itself. The logistic regression will put an enormous coefficient on it, and rightly so — *on this file*.
- **`winback_offer_sent`.** 76% of churners (2,239 of 2,939) got a retention contact that month, versus 4.5% of non-churners. The dictionary says the retention team works from an at-risk list "circulated on the third business day after month close." So this column is someone else's churn prediction, made *after* the month ended, written back into the row for that month. AUC 0.86 alone.

Now your "aren't they all zero" intuition — it's exactly right, but about the *wrong dataset*. On **April 1, 2026**, when the model scores the book, nobody has opened April's exit survey yet and no April at-risk list exists. Both columns will be 0 for everyone. In the **training file**, they're 1 on precisely the rows with label 1, because the file was assembled in March 2026 after everything had happened. That mismatch is the definition in H2 §5.2: *"the presence, in the training inputs, of information that will not be available at the moment the prediction must be made in production."* The model learns "exit survey → certain churn," gets credit for it in cross-validation (the leaked column is in the validation folds too, so CV can't catch it — §5.2 again), and then meets a book where the column is always 0 and that knowledge is worthless.

So the "cheat" isn't looking ahead a row; it's that the row itself was written after the fact. The test the professor gives: *at the exact moment I need this prediction, would this value be known, and would it have this value?* For `exit_survey_started` on April 1: known, yes; this value, no — it'd be 0 for everyone including the eventual churners.

One housekeeping point: those numbers came from a one-off check, not from `analysis/`. If you use them in the memo — and they're a strong exhibit for A2's column argument — they need to come out of the A2 script. Noting that in `NOTES.md` as an item for the A2 session.

Yes — under the dictionary's definition, that is exactly what should happen: a March cancellation is within 60 days of the end of February, so the **February** row should carry the 1, and on that row `exit_survey_started` should be 0 because nothing has happened yet. If the file were built that way, there would be no leak on that row at all — the label would be genuinely forward-looking.

The data don't do that. Two facts from the run and the check:

1. Each churner has **one** flagged row (a literal 60-day window would flag February *and* January for a March cancellation — two rows).
2. `exit_survey_started = 1` occurs **only** on flagged rows, and on 62% of them.

Put those together: the flagged row is the month in which the cancellation itself happened — the March row, in your example — not the month before. Your own reasoning is the proof: if the flagged row were February, the survey couldn't be 1 on it. So in practice the label means "cancels during this month," a roughly 30-day window starting at the snapshot, not the 60-day window after month end that the dictionary describes. I can't say *exactly* how the flag was computed without a termination date, which the extract doesn't have — but the two readings ("February flagged, March absent" vs "March flagged, February not") are the only candidates, and the exit-survey pattern rules out the first.

Why this matters, in one line: **the row is scored at the start of the month, but some of its columns describe events during that month.** The behavioural columns are safe — the dictionary defines `sessions_last_30d`, `days_since_last_login`, etc. over the 30 days "ending on the last day of the *previous* month," so on April 1 they exist. `exit_survey_started` ("opened the exit survey") and `winback_offer_sent` ("contacted about this month," from a list produced after month close) describe things that happen *after* April 1. Same row, two different clocks. That's the timing argument A2 change 1 asks for, column by column, and the rubric wants it made for the kept columns as well as the dropped ones.

Two things this changes for the memo, neither large:

- Say what the label actually is, with the evidence (one flag per churner; survey only on flagged rows). It shows you checked rather than trusted the dictionary, and it's the one fact in A1 the brief didn't hand you.
- The two-month gap in the protocol was designed for a 60-day label; with a ~30-day label it's more than sufficient. Still explain it as the brief intends — labels for June mature by end of August — and you can note it's conservative.

this reminds me of another question I had, is the analyst splitting the data into training validation and test, because there are some comments on the analyst.py that. shows what months showlud be in what partition but is it being done or is it ia note for me. also can you explain why it is the way it is

ASSISTANT  [2026-09-17T22:58:15.306Z]
==============================================================================

The windows are in [load.py:36-38](part1_northline/starter/load.py#L36-L38), not in the analyst's script, and they are **for you**. The analyst's script never touches them: it does `StratifiedKFold(shuffle=True)` on all 33,340 rows, so every fold has a random mix of January 2024 through December 2025, and there is no validation set and no test set at all. The `load.py` docstring says it plainly: "The evaluation windows below are the ones the brief tells you to use… so your A3 numbers are comparable to everyone else's — you are graded on explaining why this shape is right."

Here is the shape and why:

```
2024-01 ─────────────── 2025-06 │ 2025-07  2025-08 │ 2025-09 │ 2025-10 ── 2025-12
          Training               │   not used       │  Valid  │      Test
```

**Training ≤ 2025-06.** Fit on the past. This is H2 §5.3's rolling-origin principle: the deployed model will predict a later period from an earlier one, so the evaluation must do the same. Random folds instead let the model train on December 2025 and be tested on March 2025.

**The gap, 2025-07 and 2025-08.** This is the part people miss. A June 2025 row's label says whether the member cancelled within 60 days *after the end of June* — i.e. during July or August. So the June training rows contain outcomes that happened in July and August. If you then validated on July rows, the model would have been fitted using information from the very period it's being tested on. The gap makes sure every training label is *fully resolved* before the validation period begins: the last training month is June, its labels mature at the end of August, validation starts in September. The brief's one-sentence answer is "labels for June 2025 do not mature until end of August 2025." (Given what we found — the label behaves like a ~30-day window — the gap is more than needed. Still explain it as designed; you can add that it's conservative.)

**Validation = 2025-09.** One month for "any choice made by looking at data." That's the H2 §3.1 validation role — hyperparameters, thresholds, which columns — done as a single time-ordered holdout rather than random folds, for the same reason as above.

**Test = 2025-10 → 2025-12.** Three months, scored once, selects nothing. The final number and the A3 dollars. Note from the diagnostic that this window has corporate at 18–20% of rows, the closest the history gets to the 35% forecast — which is why change 4 (report by segment) is done on the test window.

So in A2, rows 1 and 2 (admissible columns, grouped by member) are still cross-validated on the building data because you haven't introduced time yet; from row 3 onward you use these windows, and the table has to say which is which.

ASSISTANT  [2026-09-17T23:01:00.476Z]
==============================================================================

Logically, yes: if a 1 means "cancels during this month," then June's labels are settled by June 30 and July could be validation with no overlap. The gap would be unnecessary.

But I'd keep it, for three reasons in decreasing order of weight:

1. **The brief fixes the windows** — "use these evaluation windows for the final protocol, so that everyone's numbers are comparable." Changing them makes your A3 numbers incomparable with the class's and costs marks on a point that isn't being graded on cleverness.
2. **You've inferred the label mechanics, not confirmed them.** The pattern (one flag per churner, survey only on flagged rows) is consistent with "cancels this month," but the extract has no termination date, and the team that built it documented 60 days. A protocol should be safe under the documented definition, not the one you reverse-engineered — if the analytics team is right and you're wrong, a no-gap protocol leaks; if you're right and they're wrong, the gap merely wastes two months.
3. **What the gap costs is small.** Two months of training data, roughly 4,200 of 31,645 rows, and those two months are the competitor-launch spike, which is arguably the period you'd least want to fit on anyway.

So the memo sentence stays as the brief intends — "labels for June do not mature until end of August" — and you add a clause: given that the extract flags only the cancellation month, the gap is conservative. That shows you checked, without changing the protocol.

A rule that predicts \"no\nchurn\" for everyone is therefore 90.7% accurate while identifying nobody.\nAccuracy is the wrong yardstick here for two reasons: the outcome is rare, so\nthe number is dominated by the 91% of rows where nothing happens; and the two\nmistakes cost different amounts — a wasted offer costs $18, a churner not\noffered forgoes $66 — so counting them equally is the wrong loss. The number\nthat fits the decision is the dollar value of the programme at the\ncost-derived threshold (Exhibit A3); AUC is used below only to compare\nevaluation protocols before any threshold exists.\n\nRanking members by a single behavioural column, with no model at all, gives\nan AUC between 0.65 and 0.68 (shorter sessions, fewer app opens, longer since\nlast login). Tenure on its own carries no ranking signal (0.50). The analyst's\nmodel reports 0.967.\n\n**What would have to be true for 0.967 to describe April 2026.** Four\nthings. Every column the model uses would have to exist at the moment of\nscoring — but `winback_offer_sent` is written from an at-risk list circulated\nafter month close, and `exit_survey_started` is the cancellation flow itself.\nThe April book would have to resemble the pooled 2024–25 file — but corporate\nmembers were 0% of 2024 rows, 10% of 2025 rows, 20% of December 2025, and are\nforecast at 35%. The churn process would have to be stable across the two\nyears — but the monthly rate ranged from 5.3% (April 2025) to 14.2% (August\n2025), with the two highest months coinciding with the competitor's July 2025\nlaunch. And predicting a member's month from that same member's other months\nwould have to count as prediction — which is what random folds permit. Set\nagainst a best single-column AUC of 0.68, a jump to 0.97 from a linear\ncombination of fourteen columns is the pattern the course describes as a leak\nuntil shown otherwise. Section A2 tests each of these one at a time.\n\n---\n\n## Notes to self (not memo text)\n\n### What the label actually is\n\nFrom `a1_cleaning.txt`: 2,939 churners, none with more than one flagged row,\nand every flagged row is the member's last row. The dictionary defines the\ntarget as a 60-day forward window, which would normally flag two rows per\nchurner. The extract flags one: the cancellation month itself. So in practice\n`churn_next_60d = 1` means \"this is the member's final month on the book.\"\nWorth one line in the memo (shows the dictionary was checked, not trusted).\nIt also means the brief's two-month gap between training and validation is\nconservative; keep it anyway — the brief fixes the windows for comparability,\nand a protocol should be safe under the documented definition.\n\n### Evidence for the leakage claim — NOT YET IN THE RUN\n\nThe following came from a one-off check in session 2, not from `analysis/`.\nDo not put these numbers in the memo until the A2 script reproduces them.\n\n- `exit_survey_started = 1` on 1,810 rows; every one is a churner (0 of 28,706\n  non-churner rows); all on last rows; covers 62% of churners. AUC alone 0.808.\n- `winback_offer_sent = 1` on 3,533 rows; 2,239 are churners (63%), vs 2.5%\n  churn among rows with 0; covers 76% of churners. AUC alone 0.858.\n\nMechanism, in one sentence: the row is scored at the start of the month, but\nthese two columns record events during or after it, so on 1 April 2026 they\nwill be 0 for everyone — including the members who go on to cancel — and what\nthe model learned from them is worthless at deployment.\n\n### Arguing against the draft\n\n- The 0.68 → 0.97 gap is a suspicion, not proof; fourteen columns can beat any\n  one. Keep \"until shown otherwise\"; A2 change 1 is the test.\n- Seasonality vs competitor: 2024 also peaked in Jul–Sep (12.4–13.3%). Two\n  years cannot separate the two; the draft says \"coinciding with,\" not \"caused\n  by.\" Keep it that way.\n- `tenure_months` at 0.50 is not a bug: churn may be non-monotone in tenure,\n  which a single ranking cannot capture — and a linear logit cannot either.\n- 70% of members churning over 24 months is consistent with a 9% 60-day rate\n  (a hazard of roughly 4.5%/month compounds to ~67% over 24 months).\n- Row counts differ from the analyst's (31,645 vs 33,340) because of dedup;\n  the duplicated March 2025 rows have a below-average rate (6.0%), so dedup\n  raises the base rate slightly. Diagnostic only: how do they sit against the label?\nPOST_SCORING_COLUMNS = [\"exit_survey_started\", \"winback_offer_sent\"]\n\n\ndef column_timing(df: pd.DataFrame) -> pd.DataFrame:\n    \"\"\"For each post-scoring column: how a 1 in it lines up with the label.\"\"\"\n    y = df[TARGET]\n    is_last = df.index.isin(df.groupby(\"member_id\").tail(1).index)\n    rows = []\n    for col in POST_SCORING_COLUMNS:\n        one = df[col].eq(1)\n        rows.append({\n            \"column\": col,\n            \"rows_with_1\": int(one.sum()),\n            \"share_of_rows_with_1\": round(one.mean(), 4),\n            \"churners_among_1s\": int(y[one].sum()),\n            \"non_churners_among_1s\": int((1 - y[one]).sum()),\n            \"p_churn_given_1\": round(y[one].mean(), 4),\n            \"p_churn_given_0\": round(y[~one].mean(), 4),\n            \"share_of_churners_with_1\": round(y[one].sum() / y.sum(), 4),\n            \"ones_on_members_last_row\": int((one & is_last).sum()),\n            \"auc_alone\": round(roc_auc_score(y, df[col]), 4),\n        })\n    return pd.DataFrame(rows)"

## The AUC of a 0/1 column

Same definition as always — pick a random churner and a random non-churner; AUC is the probability the churner has the higher score, with ties counting half (R1 Prop. 4.1). With a binary column the score is just 0 or 1, so the arithmetic is transparent, and you can check the 0.808 by hand:

- P(churner has 1) = 1,810 / 2,939 = 0.616; P(non-churner has 1) = 0 / 28,706 = 0.
- Churner 1, non-churner 0 → ranked correctly: 0.616 × 1 = 0.616.
- Both 0 → tie, half credit: 0.384 × 1 × ½ = 0.192.
- AUC = 0.616 + 0.192 = **0.808**.

For `winback_offer_sent`: P(churner has 1) = 0.762, P(non-churner has 1) = 1,294 / 28,706 = 0.045. Correct pairs 0.762 × 0.955 = 0.728; ties (0.762 × 0.045 + 0.238 × 0.955) × ½ = 0.131; total **0.858**. So the number is exactly "coverage of churners, plus half the ties," which is why a column that flags 62% of churners with zero false alarms lands at 0.81 rather than near 1 — the 38% of churners it doesn't flag are indistinguishable from non-churners. Worth being able to do that on paper; it's the kind of thing the midterm asks.

## Cleaned CSV — no, and that's the right call

Cleaning is dynamic: every script reads the delivered file and cleans in memory. No cleaned copy exists on disk. Three reasons that's what the assignment wants:

- CLAUDE.md: never modify a supplied data file. The checklist: don't include `data/` in the submission — the graders have it. A cleaned CSV under `analysis/outputs/` would be a 2.7 MB copy of the data in the zip.
- "Every number reproducible from one command" is satisfied by cleaning inside the run, and the cleaning steps are then visible as code the grader can read (and you can explain).
- `a1_cleaning.txt` records what the cleaning did, so the audit trail exists without the data.

What *should* change, next session: the cleaning currently lives inside `a1_baselines.py`. A2 and A3 need the identical steps, so it should move to a small shared module — `analysis/part1/prep.py` with a `load_clean()` — that every Part 1 script imports. Otherwise three scripts each dedup in their own way and one of them drifts. I'll put that in `NOTES.md` as the first task for the A2 session.

Now closing out. Order: `NOTES.md`, `sessions/README.md`, then copy and render the transcript, then commit and push.

- **B3:** one sentence each on three questions, each tied to a number.

Nothing forbids me drafting either one; what makes them yours is that the recommendation, the conditions, the column decisions, and the "what I rejected" content are decisions you made and can explain without notes. Your transcript this session shows a lot of questioning — the last-row pushback, "is that in the run?", the two-month gap — so the note should read like that: specific, slightly rough, in your voice. That's easier to write yourself from the draft bullets than to edit down from something smooth.

so why did we look at the auc of the exit surveyt awnd winback offer was there a specific reason because even without that we know that they are leakege

==============================================================================
ASSISTANT  [2026-09-17T23:45:16.886Z]
==============================================================================

[thinking]


==============================================================================
ASSISTANT  [2026-09-17T23:45:30.217Z]
==============================================================================

You're right that the decision doesn't depend on it. The professor is explicit: leakage "is a question about business process and data provenance, not about statistics, and no amount of resampling substitutes for asking it" (H2 §5.2). The timing notes in the dictionary are sufficient grounds to drop both columns, and the rubric grades the column decisions on exactly that — "argued from availability at the moment of scoring." If the memo said "we dropped them because their AUC was high," that would be the *wrong* reason: a genuinely available column with a high AUC should be kept.

So why look? Three reasons, in decreasing order of importance, and the first is honest rather than principled:

1. **You asked me why they'd leak, and I didn't want to assert it from the dictionary.** The cross-tab turned "the dictionary says these are recorded later" into "and the data behave exactly as if they were": a 1 in `exit_survey_started` is a churner every single time. That's corroboration that the timing note is true, not a substitute for it.

2. **It sizes what to expect from A2 change 1.** The brief says one of the four changes will move the number a great deal. Knowing that two columns alone rank at 0.81 and 0.86 tells you the drop when they're removed will be large, and roughly where the honest number should land relative to the 0.65–0.68 behavioural baselines. If A2 change 1 then barely moves the AUC, *that* would be the surprise to investigate.

3. **It's the third baseline in H2 Exercise 10:** "a model with the suspected-leaky features removed — if performance barely drops, the remaining signal is real; if it collapses, the 0.71 was leakage." A2 change 1 is that test; the single-column AUCs are what let you say in advance what "collapse" would look like.

For the memo, the order matters: timing argument first, as the reason; the cross-tab second, as evidence the argument holds in this extract. Not the other way round.
